Experimental Design for Data 
Science and Engineering
Theory, experiments, computation, and data are considered as the four pillars of science and 
engineering. Experimental Design for Data Science and Engineering describes efficient statistical 
methods for making the experiments cheaper and computations faster for extracting valuable 
information from data, and helps identify discrepancies in the theory. The book also includes 
recent advances in experimental designs for dealing with large amounts of observational data.
Traditionally, the design and analysis of physical and computer experiments are treated differ -
ently, but this book attempts to create a unified framework using Gaussian process models. Al -
though optimal designs are formulated using Gaussian process models, the focus is on obtaining 
practical experimental designs that are robust to model assumptions. A wide variety of topics are 
covered in the book – from designs for interpolating or integrating simple functions to designs 
that are useful for optimizing and calibrating complex computer models. It draws techniques 
that are spread across the fields of statistics, applied mathematics, operations research, uncer -
tainty quantification, and information theory, and builds experimental design as a fundamental 
data analytic tool for engineering and scientific discoveries.
•  Designs for both computer and physical experiments are discussed in a unified framework.
•  Integrates several concepts from numerical analysis, Monte Carlo methods, sensitivity analy -
sis, optimization, and machine learning with experimental design techniques in statistics.
•  Methods are explained using many real experiments from the physical sciences and engineering.
•  Experimental design techniques for analysis and compression of big data are discussed.
•  All the numerical illustrations in the book are reproducible using R and Python codes pro -
vided on the author’s GitHub site.
V . Roshan Joseph is A. Russell Chandler III Chair and Professor in the Stewart School of In -
dustrial and Systems Engineering at Georgia Institute of Technology, Atlanta. He is an author 
of more than 100 journal articles and has received many research awards. He is a Fellow of the 
American Statistical Association and the American Society for Quality, and a former editor of 
Technometrics.

Preface
An experiment can be designed to collect data and obtain the maximum information from
a system, or it may be that the data are already collected through an observational study
and we want to summarize the data without losing any information. The data can be
represented as points in the space of some variables, which can also be viewed as samples
from a probability distribution. We will call the set of points at which we want to collect
the data or the points that best summarize the data as optimal samples. This book is about
optimal sampling, which is rooted in the discipline of experimental design in statistics.
Traditionally, the field of experimental design has focused on the planning and collection
of data from physical systems. It is originated from agricultural experiments from the pio-
neering work of Ronald Fisher and expanded into science, engineering, and industry. There
are many excellent books that deal with this subject in depth such as Wu and Hamada
(2021). Recently the focus of this field has been shifted to computer experiments as many
physical systems can be modeled and simulated in computers. Santner et al. (2018) is an
excellent book that deals with design of computer experiments.
Since most computer models are deterministic in nature, the experimental designs turned
out to be closely related to the points/knots used for interpolation and integration in nu-
merical analysis, which has its own rich history. Fang and Wang (1993) is a nice book that
explains the use of Quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC) techniques in numerical integration. This
also leads to the field of operations research, where Monte Carlo techniques are extensively
used for simulation. This makes a connection to Bayesian statistics, where simulating from
probability distributions is a key technique for making inferences. However, when complex
computer codes are involved, experimental design-based techniques can become useful for
propagating uncertainties, which is a topic studied in the field of uncertainty quantification.
When optimization is the objective, the samples are gathered sequentially so that we can
move to the optimum location in the space with few evaluations of the objective function.
This topic is intimately connected to sequential experimental designs in statistics and active
learning techniques in machine learning.
Once the data are collected either through an experimental or observational study, clas-
sical methods in statistics focus on summarizing the data through sufficient statistics and
parameterized models, whereas modern trends in machine learning and nonparametric re-
gression methods is to directly model the full data. However, as the size of the data grows,
modeling can be a computational bottleneck, in which compressing the data to a smaller
size without losing information becomes useful. This topic has connections to quantization
of probability distributions, which again has a long history stemming from communications
and information theory. Although there are no experiments to perform, experimental design
concepts can still be useful for selecting the most informative points from the data.
The aim of this book is to review some of the experimental design tools and tech-
niques that are spread across the fields of statistics, numerical analysis, optimization, sim-
ulation, uncertainty quantification, and information theory. I hope that bringing several of
these concepts into one place under the unifying theme of experimental design will stim-
ulate interests from different communities and lead to research that will help advance the
field faster. Although the coverage of topics is much broader than in a typical book on
xi

--- Page 13 ---
xii Preface
experimental design, this book omits several techniques that are used in clinical trials, causal
inference, survey sampling, and online experiments. The experimental design techniques dis-
cussed in this book are mainly tailored for applications in physical sciences, engineering,
and data science.
This book is written in a somewhat unorthodox style in the sense that the presentation
of topics does not follow their historical development. They are presented in an order of
how they should have developed rather than how they got developed. For example, although
physical experiments were developed much before computer experiments, in this book, phys-
ical experiments are presented after the computer experiments. The reason for doing this is
that computer experiments are usually deterministic in nature and the data are error-free.
On the other hand, the data from physical experiments are corrupted by noise. Thus, in
principle, the data from computer experiments are cleaner and easier to handle. Therefore,
the techniques for the design and analysis of computer experiments are discussed in Part
II of the book, and then, the experimental design techniques for physical experiments are
discussed in light of the developments made in computer experiments. This will provide a
new perspective to the reader and, as I hope, will lead to new developments in the field.
I started to write this book as a research monograph, and therefore it overemphasizes
some of my own research work. However, over time I have included several topics that now
I feel this book can be genuinely used as a textbook or as a reference book for a second
course on design of experiments. In fact, the material in this book grew out of a course on
advanced experimental design that I have offered for graduate students at Georgia Tech.
A critical component of this book is the associated GitHub site which provides R codes
for reproducing all the numerical illustrations of this book:
https://github.com/vroshanjoseph/rexpdes.
I have chosen to separate the codes from the book so that a reader who is not familiar with
R programming will not be distracted by those codes. Although I have extensively cited
R functions and packages throughout the book, the methods are explained without using
any programming language. However, browsing the R codes on the GitHub site will help
improve learning. For those readers who prefer to use Python, my student Youngseo Cho
has translated several R scripts into Python and implemented Python wrappers for R-only
packages which are provided on the GitHub site:
https://github.com/vroshanjoseph/pyexpdes.
I am thankful to many individuals and organizations that have inspired and supported
me during the writing of this book. I am grateful for the support provided by my school
through the endowment of A. Russell Chandler III and many grants from the US National
Science Foundation, in particular DMS-2310637. I would like to thank Jeff Wu for his con-
stant encouragement. A special thanks goes to my collaborators Shreyes Melkote, Surya
Kalidindi, Rampi Ramprasad, William Brenneman, Bill Myers, Douglas Ray, Mark Losego,
Patrick Knapp, and William Lewis, who have exposed me to challenging experimental de-
sign problems in science and engineering. I am thankful for the comments received from
Hongquan Xu and Abhyuday Mandal, and to Bobby Gramacy for sharing his experience in
publishing a book. This work would not have been possible without the contribution and
help from my current and former students Simon Mak, Bill Huang, Shangkun Wang, Difan
Song, Wei-Yang Yu, and Youngseo Cho.
V. Roshan Joseph

Part I
Introduction

--- Page 15 ---


--- Page 16 ---
1
Experiments
1.1 Overview
An important task in science and engineering is to develop a relationship between the inputs
and outputs of a system. The “System” that we are interested in could be very general and
may refer to a rocket engine, a machining process, or even a human brain. The inputs, for
example, in a machining process, could be the speed of machining or a specific angle of the
cutting tool, while the outputs could be the force on the tool or the temperature on the
workpiece.
An approach to build the input-output relationship (also known as the response surface)
is to collect data from the system and develop the relationship using statistical and machine
learning methods. When data is expensive and resources are limited, we need to be careful
about the data collection process so that we can obtain “useful” data with minimum cost
and time. Performing carefully planned experiments on the system is one way to do this.
Here an experiment refers to making purposeful changes to the inputs and observing the
outputs as opposed to an observational study, where we simply observe both inputs and
outputs without making any changes to the system. An experimental design is a plan for
doing such an experiment, which prescribes where and how to collect the data from the
system.
Another approach to build the input-output relationship is to use the physical laws gov-
erning the system (such as Newton’s laws) and derive the relationship. One may think that
with such an approach we do not need data or experiments, but that is not true! The physics-
based models may contain unknown constants (such as friction coefficient), which can be
specified only if we have data from the physical system. Moreover, the physics-based model
may not be an easy-to-evaluate analytical function and may require sophisticated numerical
methods, such as finite element methods for solving the underlying mathematical model,
which could be a system of partial differential equations. The numerical implementation of
the solution procedure on computers is usually known as a computer model or simulator.
Since the computer model is computationally expensive, we again need to carefully plan the
computer experiments. Thus, experimental designs are important in this context as well,
but “how” to do the experiments becomes irrelevant and we only need to be concerned with
“where” to do the experiments.
In some cases, a combination of the foregoing two approaches may be needed to fully
develop the input-output relationship. For example, the investigator may not have full
understanding of the system and may be able to develop a physics-based model that is only
partially correct. This situation is very common, as most engineered or biological systems
are very complex and difficult to model completely using physics. Therefore, we will also
need to collect data from the physical system to correct for the bias due to missing physics
and to calibrate the computer model. This will require both a physical experiment and a
computer experiment.
DOI: 10.1201/9781003661535-1 3
This chapter has been made available under a CC-BY-NC-ND license 4.0.

--- Page 17 ---
4 Experiments
FIGURE 1.1: Optimization of crystal structures using Density Functional Theory. Figure
courtesy: Rampi Ramprasad’s lab.
Huang et al. (2021) described the problem of making hybrid membranes for chemical
separations. Metal particles are infused into a polymer membrane to make hybrid mem-
branes so that they do not easily dissolve during the chemical separation process. This
is done using a vapor phase infiltration process. Ren et al. (2021) developed a reaction-
diffusion model to predict the amount of metal particles trapped in the polymer membrane.
However, this model has five unknown parameters (such as diffusion constants) that need
to be specified before the model can be used for prediction. They can be estimated from
the real data shown as a black solid line in Figure 1.2. This is a model calibration problem,
but cannot be easily solved using the usual nonlinear regression techniques because here
the computer model is expensive to evaluate. Therefore, experimental design techniques are
necessary to efficiently solve this problem.
Gul et al. (2018) reported a problem faced by a machining software company. The right
side of Figure 1.3 shows the tangential force acting on the tool over time in a solid end
milling simulation. Some of the inputs that can be varied in the machining software console
are shown on the left side of the figure. The simulator is deterministic; that is, it will give
the same output of tangential force for the same input settings. However, in reality, the tool
wears out and the material properties change from piece to piece. Therefore, the output
force will not be exactly as predicted and will have some variability depending on the state
1.2 Applications
Once the input-output relationship is developed, it can be used for many purposes, such as 
optimizing or controlling the system. Below are a few real examples.
Krishna et al. (2024) were interested in finding stable crystal structures. However, cre-
ating materials in a lab with different crystal structures and investigating their stability 
is tedious, expensive, and time consuming. Crystal structures that are found in nature are 
known to have low potential energy. Therefore, a more feasible approach would be to com-
pute the potential energy of a crystal structure using Density Functional Theory (DFT) 
and find the optimal atomic configuration that minimizes the potential energy (Figure 1.1). 
Although this computational approach is cheaper, it is still not an easy problem because 
DFT computations are expensive and the atomic space to explore is huge. To add to that 
complexity, the potential energy surface can have many local minima (e.g., graphite and di-
amond are different configurations of carbon atoms). Experimental design techniques can be 
used to efficiently search the huge atomic space and find the global optimum with minimum 
number of DFT computations.

--- Page 18 ---
Applications 5
FIGURE 1.2: Calibration of reaction-diffusion model in a vapor-phase infiltration process.
The real data is shown as a black solid line and three model evaluations as dashed lines.
Reprinted with permission from Yi Ren, Emily K. McGuinness, Chaofan Huang, V. Roshan
Joseph, Ryan P. Lively, and Mark D. Losego (2021). “Reaction–Diffusion Transport Model
to Predict Precursor Uptake and Spatial Distribution in Vapor-Phase Infiltration Pro-
cesses”. In: Chemistry of Materials 33(13), 5210–5222. Copyright 2021 American Chemical
Society.
of the tool and the material. The software company would like to add a confidence interval
to the prediction to capture the uncertainty in the inputs. A simple way to construct this
confidence interval is to use Monte Carlo (MC). That is, we can draw random samples from
the distribution of the inputs and then run the simulator to produce a collection of the
FIGURE 1.3: Uncertainty propagation in a solid end milling process. Figure courtesy:
Shreyes Melkote’s lab.

--- Page 19 ---
6 Experiments
output curves from which we can obtain, say, the 95% confidence intervals. This confidence
interval will be accurate if we use a large number of MC samples. However, this is inefficient
because the simulation is not cheap to run. We can use experimental design techniques to
optimally sample from the input distribution and propagate the uncertainty, thus saving
the simulation time.
The objectives of the aforementioned examples are different: The first problem on crystal
structures is about optimization, the second problem on membranes is about calibration, and
the third problem on machining is about uncertainty propagation. There is no single optimal
experimental design solution to all these problems. In fact, the optimal experimental design
differs depending on the objective and the type of response surface. Figure 1.4 illustrates
several examples using one-dimensional functions. For example, suppose that the objective
is to approximate the response surface. If the response surface is rough, then a design that
uniformly fills the space is good (top left panel of Figure 1.4); whereas, if the function is
smooth, then a design that places more points near the boundary is good (top right panel).
If the objective is to maximize a function, we should place more design points near its global
maxima (bottom-left panel). On the other hand, if the objective is to propagate uncertainty,
the design points should be placed in such a way as to mimic the distribution of the input
(bottom right panel with function depicting the density of input).
approximate a rough function approximate a smooth function
optimization uncertainty propagation
FIGURE 1.4: Optimal experimental design (solid circles) for different objectives.
An experimental design specifies where the data needs to be collected. Therefore, when
trying to find the optimal experimental design, we do not have any data with us. In other
words, we do not know anything about the response surface when designing the experiment.

--- Page 20 ---
Role of Uncertainty 7
But Figure 1.4 shows that the optimal experimental design depends on the underlying
response surface (except for the last case). Thus, finding the optimal experimental design
seems like an ill-defined problem. Fortunately, there is one way to get around this issue. We
do not need to specify all the design points beforehand. We can start with an initial design,
collect data, and then decide where to do the next experiment. This is called a sequential
design approach (also known as active learning in machine learning literature). As far as the
initial design is concerned, the uniformly spread points shown in the top left panel of Figure
1.4 seem like a good strategy. This is called a space-filling design and will be discussed in
detail in a later chapter.
1.3 Role of Uncertainty
If we know everything about the underlying response surface, then we do not need to collect
data or need any experiments. It is the uncertainty about the response surface that forces
us to perform experiments and collect data. The data bring us new information and reduce
our uncertainty about the response surface. Thus, it makes sense to perform experiments
that maximize information or minimize uncertainty. As shown in Lindley (1956), the theory
of optimal experimental design can be formulated using concepts from information theory
(Shannon, 1948). However, we can develop most of the experimental design techniques
discussed in this book without delving into information theory as long as we have a way to
quantify uncertainties. We will quantify the uncertainties using some statistical modeling
techniques.
To discuss about modeling techniques, we need to first introduce some notation. Let
x = (x1, . . . , xp)′ be the p inputs (also known as factors) and y be the output (or response).
There could be many outputs, but in most cases it is enough to develop the modeling
techniques for multi-input/single-output system, which can be separately applied on each
of the outputs. Assume the statistical model
y = h(x) + ϵ, (1.1)
where h(x) is the input-output relationship (response surface) and ϵ is the random error
caused by changes in numerous other factors that might affect the output but not considered
in the experiment. In a physical experiment, ϵ will also include the measurement errors
caused by the instrument. If the experiment is performed using a deterministic computer
model, we can ignore the term ϵ. It is common to assume ϵ ∼ N(0, σ2), where σ2 is the
variance of the random error. The normality assumption and the additive error assumption
in (1.1) may not be valid, and some transformations may be needed to make it work in
practice (Box and Cox, 1964).
Suppose that we performed an experiment with one factor at two values (also known
as levels): x = 0 and x = 1 with three replicates. Data are shown as solid blue circles
in Figure 1.5. Assume h(·) to be the function shown as a black solid line in the figure,
but unknown to the experimenter. Linear regression models are commonly used in physical
experiments (Wu and Hamada, 2021). Suppose that we fit the model yi = β0 + β1xi + ϵi
to the data, where ϵi
iid
∼ N(0, σ2), for i = 1, . . . , n(here n = 6). The fitted line along with
95% confidence intervals are shown in the left panel of the figure. Gaussian process (GP)
regression (Rasmussen and Williams, 2006) is rarely used in this context, but is also shown
in the right panel of the same figure.
Although the predictions from both the linear and GP regressions are approximately the
same, the prediction intervals are completely different. This is because of the way these two

--- Page 21 ---
8 Experiments
FIGURE 1.5: Comparison of linear and Gaussian process regression.
types of model quantify the prediction uncertainties, and it is important for us to understand
their differences. The uncertainty of linear regression prediction includes the uncertainties in
the estimated parameters ( ˆβ0 and ˆβ1) and the random error (ϵ). The uncertainty is highest
at the extremes (x = 0 and x = 1) and smallest at the middle (x = 0.5). On the other hand,
GP regression is a nonparametric regression method. Therefore, its prediction uncertainty
includes the uncertainties about the function h(·) and the random error. It has minimum
uncertainty where we have observed the data and maximum uncertainty in the middle. This
is completely opposite of linear regression!
Clearly, the uncertainty estimate from the GP regression makes more sense than that
of the linear regression. The GP regression uncertainty estimate would suggest that we
perform the next experiment in the middle, which will give us more information about the
true function. On the other hand, the linear regression uncertainty estimate would suggest
that we continue to perform experiments at extremes. This is because the linear regression
uncertainty estimate is obtained by assuming that y = β0 + β1x is the true model. Thus,
not being able to capture the uncertainties in the model is a major weakness of the linear
regression approach.
Most of the experimental designs on physical experiments are developed by assuming
linear regression models (Box et al., 1978; Montgomery, 2017; Wu and Hamada, 2021).
Does this mean that they are not useful? Not really! When the number of factors ( p) is high
and when the number of experiments (n) is small, the GP regression will also give larger
uncertainties at the boundaries coinciding with the results of the linear regression. Most
physical experiments are of this kind (large p and small n); therefore, using a linear or GP
regression framework would likely produce identical results. However, relying on the linear
regression framework does impose some limitations in our quest for optimal designs.
Suppose that we have six continuous factors to experiment and a budget of about 30
experiments (also known as runs). One could consider a two-level fractional factorial design
26−1, which requires 32 runs. Alternatively, we could consider using a three-level fractional
factorial design 3 6−3 which requires 27 runs or a central composite design with 29 runs (12
axial points, 16 cube points, and 1 center point). The literature on experimental design is
not clear on which of these designs should be used. This is because each of them assumes

--- Page 22 ---
Outline of the Book 9
a certain linear regression model and does not entertain the possibility of misspecifications
of the model. The need to obtain designs that are robust to model misspecifications has
long been recognized in the literature. However, the inability of linear regression models to
fully capture the model uncertainties limits their usefulness. For example, one can obtain
a 2 6−1 minimum aberration design (Fries and Hunter, 1980), which is considered to be a
model robust design (Cheng et al., 1999). However, it is robust only to a linear regression
model that includes linear effects, and two-factor and higher-order interactions; it does not
consider the possibility of nonlinear effects.
The foregoing discussion suggests that we would be better off developing the optimal
design framework using GP regression than linear regression because of GP’s ability to
better quantify model uncertainties. This is the approach taken in this book.
1.4 Outline of the Book
Due to the reliance of GP models in many of the experimental design techniques discussed
in the book, we will start with a gentle introduction to GP modeling in Chapter 2. However,
before introducing GP, we start with the more familiar polynomial interpolation and then
transition slowly to GP after a gentle introduction to radial basis functions and kriging.
Extensions of these approaches to noisy data are also discussed, which become essential for
the methods in Parts II and IV. Some important concepts in experimental design are also
introduced during their discussion.
Part II of the book starts with GP-based experimental design methods. Chapter 3 is
short because those methods are not very useful in practice because of their dependence
on unknown hyperparameters of the GP model. However, Chapter 3 forms the basis for
developing the more practical and useful experimental design methods in Chapters 4–7.
Chapter 4 discusses some of the key concepts of the book on space-filling designs, which
are motivated as the limiting cases of GP model-based designs, but also possess geomet-
rical interpretations. They can be considered as model-robust designs, and thus useful for
practical applications. Chapter 5 extends these ideas to non-uniform distributions, which
lays the foundations for the data science methods discussed in Part IV. Chapter 6 discusses
a very useful special case of space-filling design where the interest is in quickly screening
factors. This chapter also introduces some useful concepts from global sensitivity analysis
that form the basis for variable selection techniques discussed later in Chapter 11. Chapter
7 develops sequential design methods to achieve a specific objective, such as optimization,
where the designs from Chapters 4 to 6 can be used as initial designs.
Chapter 8 in Part III on fractional factorial designs is kept intentionally short because of
the availability of many excellent textbooks on this topic. However, this chapter can still be
interesting to the readers because it tries to connect with many of the experimental design
and modeling methods introduced in Parts I and II. Chapter 9 discusses the case where we
have data from both computer and physical experiments.
Part IV shows the application of experimental design methods in subsampling (Chapter
10) and data analysis (Chapter 11). Experimental design is traditionally viewed in statistics
as a technique for data collection and planning. This part is included in the book to create
an appreciation of the broad applicability of design of experiments and reinforce it as a
foundational topic of statistics.

--- Page 23 ---
2
Modeling Techniques
As mentioned in the Introduction, experiments will be performed to reduce our uncertainty
about the response surface, and we need to use statistical modeling techniques to quantify
the uncertainties. The main technique that we will use is Gaussian Process (GP) regression,
but before explaining about it we will examine a few other closely related modeling concepts:
polynomial models and radial basis functions from the numerical analysis literature and
kriging from the geostatistics literature. As we will soon see, all of these modeling techniques
will lead us to a very similar solution, but learning about these seemingly different ideas
will give us new perspectives about the problem which will be useful to us in later chapters.
To develop these ideas, first we will assume that the data are not corrupted by random
error; that is, there is no ϵ in (1.1). This situation is common with deterministic computer
models, such as the DFT in Figure 1.1 or the machining simulator in Figure 1.3. Because of
the absence of random errors, we are looking for models that can interpolate the data. This
is discussed in Section 2.1. Then, in Section 2.2, we will extend these interpolating models
to deal with noisy data.
2.1 Interpolation
The interpolating models discussed in this section will be used extensively in Part II of the
book on computer experiments.
2.1.1 Polynomial Interpolation
Consider a one-dimensional test function
h(x) = sin(10πx)
1 + 64(x − .25)2 + x2, x ∈ [0, 1]. (2.1)
We will assume that this function is unknown to the experimenter and has access to only
some data. Suppose that the data are generated by doing n = 10 experiments, where the
experimental design is an equi-spaced set of 10 points in [0, 1]. The test function (black
dashed line) and the data {(xi, yi)}10
i=1 (blue dots) are plotted on the left panel of Figure
2.1.
We can interpolate the n data points using an (n − 1)th degree polynomial:
y = β0 + β1x + . . .+ βn−1xn−1. (2.2)
There are explicit solutions to one-dimensional polynomial interpolation such as the La-
grange interpolation formula. However, to connect the interpolation formula with the other
methods discussed in this chapter, we will take a different approach.

First, rewrite the polynomial in (2.2) as

does not seem to be a good approximation to the true function except for the middle region.
The interpolator fluctuates wildly at the two ends, which is a known issue with high-degree
polynomial interpolation called Runge’s phenomenon.

Although the use of Chebyshev nodes as the experimental design for polynomial inter-
polation in one-dimensional problems seems to work well, polynomial interpolation has an
issue when there is more than one input variable. Consider the 2 2 full factorial design (Wu
and Hamada, 2021) shown in the left panel of Figure 2.2. Note that the 2 2 design can be
obtained by crossing the 2-point design on each variable: (0 , 1) ⊗ (0, 1). We can obtain the
interpolation by fitting a polynomial model of the form
y = β0 + β1x1 + β2x2 + β3x1x2,
where the term x1x2 represents the interaction between the two inputs. The terms in this
polynomial can also be identified using a similar product representation: (1 , x1) ⊗ (1, x2) =
(1, x1, x2, x1x2). Now consider a slight perturbation of two points shown in the right panel of
the same figure. A polynomial to interpolate these four points is no longer obvious. In fact,

the interpolating polynomials may not be unique and may require specialized algorithms to

An approach to using polynomial models in higher-dimensional problems is to sample
n points from each dimension using Chebyshev nodes and then form a full factorial design.
But this would require np experiments, which is impractical when p is large. We can reduce
the number of experiments using fractional factorial designs (Chapter 8) or sparse grids
(Smolyak, 1963; Xiu, 2010; Plumlee, 2014), but such designs and the corresponding poly-
nomials are not extensible for sequential designs (Chapter 7). In sequential experiments, we
may want to add points in certain regions of the space without following any specialized
structure for the design. This necessitates more flexible interpolation methods that can work
on scattered data. Radial basis functions discussed in the next section is one such technique.
2.1.2 Radial Basis F unctions
Given the design D = {x1, . . . ,xn} and the corresponding function evaluations yi = h(xi),
i = 1, . . . , n, we are interested in obtaining an interpolator of the form
hn(x) =
nX
i=1
βiRi(x),
where Ri(x) are some basis functions of x, i = 1 , . . . , n. For one-dimensional polynomial
interpolation, we took R1(x) = 1, R2(x) = x, . . .,Rn(x) = xn−1. However, the Mairhuber-
Curtis theorem (Fasshauer, 2007, p. 3) tells us that we cannot fix the set of basis functions
in advance for interpolating arbitrary scattered data in Rp for p ≥ 2. This implies that the
basis functions must depend on the data sites. Therefore, let
Ri(x) = R (x − xi) ,
where R : Rp → R.
The interpolation condition gives rise to the following n equations
nX
j=1
βjR(xi − xj) = yi, i = 1, . . . , n.
In matrix notation, this system of equations can be written as Rβ = y, where R is the
n × n matrix with ijth element R(xi − xj), β = (β1, . . . , βn)′, and y = (y1, . . . , yn)′. If R is

--- Page 27 ---
14 Modeling Techniques
nonsingular, then we obtain the unique solution ˆβ = R−1y. This gives us the radial basis
function (RBF) interpolator
ˆy(x) = r(x)′R−1y, (2.6)
where r(x) is an n × 1 vector with ith element R(x − xi). When p = 1, this is exactly
the same as the polynomial interpolator in (2.4) except that we use radial basis functions
instead of polynomial basis functions. However, unlike the polynomial interpolator, the RBF
interpolator works for any p and does not require any structure for the design D.
Before proceeding further, we need to answer an important question. Is R invertible for
any choice of R(·) and any design D? It turns out that the exact mathematical conditions
on R(·) to make R invertible are still unknown. However, characterizations on R(·) are
available to make R a positive definite matrix, and we know that a positive definite matrix
can be inverted. For this, we need to understand the notion of a positive definite function
(Fasshauer, 2007, Ch. 3) 1.
Definition 2.1 A continuous function R : Rp → R is positive semi-definite on Rp if
nX
i=1
nX
j=1
cicjR(xi − xj) ≥ 0 (2.7)
for any design D = {x1, . . . ,xn} and for all c = (c 1, . . . , cn)′. The function R(·) is called
positive definite if (2.7) is 0 only for c = 0.
Thus, if we choose R(·) to be a positive definite function, then R will be invertible
provided that the data sites x1, . . . ,xn are distinct. The literature on RBF further restricts
R(·) to be a radial function (Fasshauer, 2007, Ch. 2).
Definition 2.2 A function R : Rp → R is called radial provided there exists a function
ψ : R+ → R such that R(x) = ψ(∥x∥), where ∥·∥ is usually the Euclidean norm.
There are many choices for the radial basis functions, and we refer to the book by Fasshauer
(2007) for details. An example is the Gaussian function
R(x) = exp

so good with θ = 1 (similar to the left panel of Figure 2.1) and very poor with θ = 0.01.
In summary, RBF interpolation is very flexible and can give excellent results, but it comes
with the additional complexity of properly tuning the length-scale parameter in the radial
basis function.

A disadvantage of RBF interpolation is that it does not provide an uncertainty estimate
for the prediction. Uncertainty estimation is important for us to decide where to do the
next experiment. In the next section, we develop a probabilistic formulation of the problem
which will provide identical predictions to those of RBF but with uncertainty estimates.
2.1.3 Kriging
Kriging is a multivariate interpolation technique named after Krige (1951) which was devel-
oped into the field of geostatistics by the pioneering work of Matheron (1963). See Cressie
(1990) for its origins. Applications of kriging to spatial statistics problems are discussed in
Cressie (1993) and its theoretical foundations in Stein (1999). The technique was brought
to the field of computer experiments by Sacks et al. (1989).
The main idea of kriging is to view the deterministic function h(x) as a realization of a
stochastic process Y (x). Let the stochastic process has a constant mean µ and covariance
function K(u, v) = cov{Y (u), Y(v)}. For a second-order stationary stochastic process, the
covariance function can be written as
K(u, v) = τ 2R(u − v),
where τ 2 is the variance and R(·) the correlation function. What correlation functions
can we use? Since cor{Y (u), Y(u)} = 1, we must have R(0) = 1. Moreover, since
cor{Y (u), Y(v)} = cor{Y (v), Y(u)}, we must have R(u − v) = R(v − u). Thus, R(·)
must be an even function. Finally, var{c′Y} ≥0 for any c, i.e., we must have c′Rc ≥ 0.
This implies that R(·) should be a positive semi-definite function. There are many functions
that satisfy these conditions; Gaussian function in (2.8) is being one of them. However, there
is no need to use functions that are radial (or isotropic). In fact, having different length-
scale parameter for each input can be very advantageous. It can be shown that products of
positive definite functions are also positive definite, which follows from a result in matrix al-
gebra that Hadamard products of positive definite matrices are positive definite (Fasshauer,
2007, p. 30). Therefore, define the Gaussian product correlation function as
R(∆) =
pY

functions by taking products as in (2.11) or replacing ∆ with a weighted Euclidean norm

the stochastic process. For example, the Gaussian correlation function leads to infinitely
differentiable sample paths, whereas the sample paths with exponential correlation function
are nowhere differentiable. See Rasmussen and Williams (2006, Ch. 4) and Fasshauer and
McCourt (2015, Ch. 3) for details.

i=1, we can form a linear predictor
ˆy(x) = a(x)′y
and try to find the best linear predictor by minimizing the mean squared prediction error:
M SE(x) = E {a(x)′Y − Y (x)}2 , (2.12)

E {a(x)′Y} = µ for all x.

the residuals and add it back to ˆµ.

2.1.4 Gaussian Process Modeling
Gaussian process (GP) provides a Bayesian interpretation of the kriging methodology pre-
sented in the previous section. It was brought to the field of computer experiments by Currin
et al. (1991), but it has been used previously in other settings (Wahba, 1978; O’Hagan, 1978).
The book by Rasmussen and Williams (2006) was influential for its application in machine
learning. The use of GPs for computer experiments is detailed in the books of Santner et al.
(2018) and Gramacy (2020).
Definition 2.3 A random function Y (x) is a Gaussian process if for any choice of
x1, . . . ,xn and any n ≥ 1, Y = ( Y (x1), . . . , Y(xn))′ has a multivariate normal distribu-
tion.

--- Page 33 ---
20 Modeling Techniques
Recall that a n-dimensional multivariate normal distribution is determined by its mean
µ ∈ Rn and covariance matrix Σ ∈ Rn×n. Thus, if y ∼ Nn(µ, Σ), then it has the density
f (y) = 1
(2π)n/2|Σ|1/2 exp

− 1
2 (y − µ)′Σ−1(y − µ)

.
GP can be viewed as a continuous extension of a multivariate normal distribution, which
is determined by a mean function µ(x) and a covariance function or kernel K(x, ·), where
µ(x) = E{Y (x)} and K(x, u) = cov{Y (x), Y(u)}.
We are interested in estimating the true input-output relationship y = h(x) from the
data {(xi, yi)}n
i=1. In a Bayesian approach, we would proceed by postulating a prior dis-
tribution for the function h(x) and then find the posterior distribution of h(x) given the
data. The posterior distribution will give us an improved understanding of the functionh(x)
compared to our prior understanding of it before seeing the data. We will use GP as a prior
distribution of h(x), that is,
h(x) ∼ GP (µ(x), K(x, ·)). (2.17)
For most of the developments in this book, we will assume that µ(x) = µ (a constant)
and K(x, u) = τ 2R(x − u), where τ 2 is the variance and R(·) the correlation function. A
few realizations of the GP for a one-dimensional input variable with R(x) = e−x2/θ2
and
θ = 0.08 are shown in the left panel of Figure 2.5. Suppose we evaluated the function and
obtained the data, which are shown in the middle panel of the same figure as blue dots.
The aim is to obtain the posterior distribution of the function given the data.

Some realizations of this GP with θ = 0.08 are also plotted in Figure 2.5 (right panel). We
can see that all these realizations pass through the data points, and the overall uncertainty
about h(x) has been substantially reduced compared to the prior distribution.
The (hyper) parameters µ, τ 2, and θ must be specified before GP can be used for
prediction. The Bayesian formulation gives us a principled way to estimate them. One
simple approach is to use empirical Bayes methods. The marginal distribution of the data
is given by

will illustrate the hierarchical Bayes approach by placing a prior only on µ. For a more
complete treatment, see Santner et al. (2018, Ch. 4).

2.2 Regression
Now we will consider the case of data corrupted by random noise. Noise is common in
physical experiments (Wu and Hamada, 2021) and stochastic simulations (Kleijnen, 2008).
For such noisy data, we are looking for models that approximate rather than interpolate.
These techniques will be used extensively in Parts III and IV of the book.
2.2.1 Linear Regression
Linear regression is one of the main data analysis tools in statistics and there are many
excellent textbooks on this subject (Faraway, 2015, 2016). Therefore, we will only give a
gentle introduction here so as to introduce some notation and concepts useful for developing
experimental designs.
Consider again the same one-dimensional function as in (2.1), but now the data are
generated by
yi = h(xi) + ϵi, i= 1, . . . , n, (2.29)
where ϵi is the random error caused by changes in the other unobserved variables or due
to measurement errors. Suppose that the data are generated by doing n = 20 experiments,
where the experimental design is an equi-spaced set of 10 points in [0, 1] with two replicates.
The test function (black dashed line) and the data {(xi, yi)}20
i=1 (blue dots) are plotted on
Figure 2.6.
Suppose that we are interested in approximating h(x) using a polynomial
y = β0 + β1x + . . .+ βmxm. (2.30)

Linear regression approach takes this as the true model and assumes that the data is gen-

instead of (2.29). Thus, in this approach, we ignore the bias caused by the polynomial
approximation to h(x), which has important consequences in terms of uncertainty quantifi-
cation. Note that although (2.31) captures nonlinear relationship, it is still called a linear
regression model in statistics because it is linear in the parameters β = (β0, . . . , βm)′. These
parameters can be chosen so that the estimated model is as close to the data as possible.
Many loss functions can be used to measure this “closeness”, the simplest of which is the
squared error loss, which is the original method proposed by Legendre and Gauss. See Plack-
ett (1972) for a fascinating history behind the method of least squares. Thus, the problem
is to

equating to zero, we obtain
X′Xβ = X′y.
If X′X is nonsingular, then we have a unique solution
ˆβ = (X′X)−1X′y. (2.32)
Since we have only 10 unique points in x in our one-dimensional example, we must have
m ≤ 9 to make the columns of X linearly independent.
The predictors ˆy(x) = x′ ˆβ, where x = (1, x, . . . , xm)′, with m = 3 and m = 8 are plotted
as solid green lines in Figure 2.6. The third-degree polynomial fit causes a large bias at the

the third-degree polynomial appears to be as good or even better than the eighth-degree

third-degree polynomial fit are narrower compared to those of the eighth-degree polynomial
fit. However, there is more bias in the third-degree polynomial fit near the observed data
points; therefore, these narrow confidence intervals do not indicate that they give a more
accurate prediction. This discrepancy in the uncertainty quantification is due to the wrong
assumption we made earlier in (2.31) that the third-degree polynomial is the true model.
This is a major drawback of the linear regression models, which we can overcome using the
nonparametric regression models discussed in the next two subsections.
Before proceeding further, let us investigate whether we can improve the linear regression
fit using experimental design techniques. The estimate of β will be accurate if the variance
of ˆβ is “small”. Since σ2 is a constant, this amounts to minimizing the matrix ( X′X)−1.
To minimize it, we can define some meaningful scalar criteria (Wald, 1943; Elfving, 1952;
Kiefer and Wolfowitz, 1960). A commonly used criterion is the determinant of (X ′X)−1,
which quantifies the volume of the confidence ellipsoid around ˆβ. Thus, to find the optimal
design
min
D
|(X′X)−1| = min
D
1/|X′X|
≡ max

|X′X|. (2.34)
This is called a D-optimal design, where “D” stands for determinant. Another popular
criterion is to minimize the sum of variances of ˆβi’s, that is, the trace of the matrix (X′X)−1:

D


(X′X)−1

Such a design is called an A-optimal design. The preceding two criteria focus on the accurate
estimation of β, which should indirectly help to improve the prediction. We can also define
criteria that directly target prediction. A G-optimal design can be obtained by minimizing
the maximum prediction variance over the entire domain X :

26 Modeling Techniques
More details of optimal designs can be found in the books Pukelsheim (1993); Silvey (1980);
Atkinson et al. (2007), and L´ opez-Fidalgo (2023). A more practical treatment of the subject
is given in Goos and Jones (2011).
Optimization of the foregoing design criteria is not easy because the objective function is
nonlinear, multimodal, and expensive to compute. Therefore, general purpose optimization
algorithms do not perform well in this problem. The most popular approach to solve them
is to use some kind of exchange algorithm; that is, start with an initial design D and a set
of candidates C, and exchange a row in D with a row in C if there is an improvement in
the objective function. Such algorithms converge to a local optimum, and therefore several
initial designs are needed to find the globally optimal design. Here we will explain the
Fedorov exchange algorithm (Fedorov, 1972) for finding D-optimal designs. See Cook and
Nachtsheim (1980) and Nguyen and Miller (1992) for details of other exchange algorithms.
Let xi be the ith row of X, that is, X = (x ′
1, . . . ,x′
n)′. Then, X′X = Pn
i=1 xix′
i.

a new model matrix Xnew, then the D-optimal objective function becomes |X′

matrix results will be useful to simplify the computations, where the second is called the
Sherman-Morrison matrix identity.
Lemma 2.4 If A is an m × m matrix and a is an m × 1 vector, then
|A + aa′| = |A|(1 + a′A−1a) and
(A + aa′)−1 = A−1 − A−1aa′A−1

much cheaper than using |X′X + cc′ − xix′

no improvement. The algorithm is implemented in the R package AlgDesign (Wheeler and
Braun, 2024).

points are pushed toward the boundaries. The two polynomial fits are better than before,
and in particular, the high fluctuations of the eighth-degree polynomial near the boundaries
have disappeared.

In Chapter 5, we will discuss experimental designs to represent a distribution, but for the
moment we will use a simple approach. It is well known that {.5/n, 1.5/n, . . . ,(n − .5)/n}
is the best set of n points to approximate a uniform distribution in [0, 1]. Then, the best set
of points to approximate the arcsine density can be obtained using the inverse probability
transform method (Section 5.2.1). For x ∈ [−1, 1], the distribution function can be obtained
as
F (x) =
Z x
−1
1
π
√
1 − x2 dx = 1 − 1
π cos−1x,
which gives F −1(x) = cos( π(1 − x)). Since {(i − .5)/n}n
i=1 or {(n − i + .5)/n}n
i=1 is the
best point set of a uniform distribution, the representative points of the arcsine density can
be obtained using the inverse probability transform as {F −1([n − i + .5]/n)}n
i=1, which is
exactly the Chebyshev nodes given in (2.5). In other words, for largen, the D-optimal design
points for fitting an (n − 1)th degree polynomial can be well approximated using Chebyshev
nodes. This makes a fascinating connection between the two fields of experimental design
and approximation theory.
Now consider the case with more than one input variable. A linear regression model that
includes only the main effects of the variables can be written as
y = β0 + β1x1 + . . .+ βpxp + ϵ.

--- Page 41 ---
28 Modeling Techniques
We can also include the polynomial terms x2
j , x3
j , ...for j = 1, . . . , p, two-factor interaction
terms xixj, xix2
j , . . ., and higher-order interaction terms xixjxk, . . .. Thus, the total number
of effects that can be included in the linear regression model, P , can be very large. In fact,
P can be much larger than the size of the data n. Let X be the n × P model matrix.
Then, rank(X′X) = n < P, and therefore X′X cannot be inverted to get the least squares
estimate as in (2.32). We can overcome this issue using ridge regression (Hoerl and Kennard,
1970). Assume that the columns of X and y are standardized to have mean 0 and standard
deviation 1. Now consider the regression model without intercept. Then, ridge regression
solves the optimization problem:
min
β
(y − Xβ)′(y − Xβ) + λβ′β, (2.39)
where λ >0 is a regularization parameter. This gives the solution
ˆβ = (X′X + λIP )−1 X′y. (2.40)
The regularization parameter λ can be chosen using cross-validation methods. The following
result is useful for deriving leave-one-out cross-validation errors. LetL = X(X′X+λIP )−1X′
be the hat matrix. Then the leave-one-out cross-validation error defined yi − ˆy∼i can be
computed using the following short-cut formula (Wasserman, 2006, p.70).
Lemma 2.5 For a linear predictor ˆy = Ly, the leave-one-out cross-validation error can be
computed using
cvi = yi − ˆyi
1 − li
, i= 1, . . . , n,
where li = Lii is the hat value or leverage.
Thus, the mean squared cross-validation error is given by M SCV = Pn
i=1 cv2
i /n. Golub
et al. (1979) proposed an approximation to MSCV called generalized cross-validation (GCV)
given by
GCV = 1
n
Pn
i=1(yi − ˆyi)2

where ¯l = Pn
i=1 li/n = tr{(X′X + λIP )−1X′X}/n. GCV has some nice properties, such
as the rotational invariance of the model matrix. Thus, λ can be estimated from data by
minimizing either the MSCV or GCV.
However, ridge regression in (2.40) contains only a single regularization parameter λ,
which indirectly assumes that main effects, two-factor interactions, etc. to be of the same
importance. This is rarely true in practice. Therefore, we need to make some modifications
to the ridge regression formulation to make it useful for the analysis of experiments. For this,
we need to first introduce certain experimental design principles such as effect hierarchy and
heredity (Wu and Hamada, 2021). We will do this later in Chapter 8 when we discuss the
analysis of physical experiments.
2.2.2 Kernel Ridge Regression
Instead of using polynomial basis functions, we could use radial basis functions as in Section
2.1.2 to approximate the underlying function. This leads to the kernel ridge regression. The
kernel K(·, ·) does not have to be radial, but should be symmetric and positive definite,
that is, K(u, v) = K(v, u) and satisfy Definition 2.1. Although we will mainly use the shift-
invariant kernelsK(u, v) = R(u−v) in this book, we will describe the kernel ridge regression

--- Page 42 ---
Regression 29
for general type of kernels. Thus, given the noisy data{(xi, yi)}n
i=1 we are interested in fitting
the model
yi =
nX
j=1
cjK(xi, xj) + ϵi,
where ϵi
iid
∼ N(0, σ2) and yi’s are assumed to be centered. This leads to the least squares
formulation:
min
c
(y − Kc)′(y − Kc),
where K is the n × n matrix with ijth element K(xi, xj) and c = (c 1, . . . , cn)′. However,
as we have seen in Section 2.1.2, this problem has a unique solution ˆc = K−1y, provided
the xi’s are distinct. This will make the predictor an interpolator, which is probably not
desirable for noisy data because the predictor can have unnecessarily large fluctuations and
can lead to poor approximation to the true underlying function. We can avoid this problem
through regularization; that is, we will add a penalty term to the least squares objective
function that controls the fluctuations of the predictor. Let hn(x) = k(x)′c be the predictor,
where k(x) is the n×1 vector with ith element K(x, xi). Thus, we wish to solve the problem
min
c
(y − Kc)′(y − Kc) + λ∥hn∥2,
where λ >0 and ∥hn∥ is a norm of the function hn(·) that controls its fluctuations.
To define a proper norm for the function hn(·), we need to first define a class of functions
in which hn(·) is a member. Consider a class of functions formed by linear span of the kernel:
h(·) =
mX
i=1
αiK(·, ui),
where m can be any positive integer and uis can be any points in the domain X . Define an

⟨f, g⟩ =
mX
i=1

space of functions H. This is called a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) because of
the reproducing property ⟨K(·, u), K(·, v)⟩ = K(u, v). See Fasshauer and McCourt (2015,
Ch.2) for details of RKHS.
Thus, the norm of the predictor hn(·) = Pn

Although this optimization appears difficult to solve, optimizing (2.41) is easy. Differenti-

By comparing to the ridge regression solution in (2.40), it should now be clear why this

special case, let K(u, v) = r(u)′r(v), where r(u) = (u, u2, . . . , um)′. Then K = RR′, where

min

which reduces to (2.39) by letting β = R′c.
The kernel ridge regression predictor ˆ y(x) = k(x)′ˆc can be used only after specifying
the regularization parameter λ and the parameters θ in the kernel function. We can again

are much better than the polynomial regressions shown in Figures 2.6 and 2.7.

The main difference between Gaussian process regression and Gaussian process interpo-
lation discussed in Section 2.1.4 is that the correlation matrix R is replaced with R + λI. In
fact, even for noiseless data, this modification using a small λ is useful for numerical stabil-
ity (Ranjan et al., 2011; Gramacy and Lee, 2012; Peng and Wu, 2014). In the geostatistics
literature, λ is called a nugget.
Consider again the 20 data points generated using the one-dimensional test function in
(2.1). We fit the Gaussian process regression using the R package rkriging (Huang and
Joseph, 2024) with a Gaussian correlation function R(u − v) = exp{−(u − v)2/θ2}. The
predictions and 95% confidence intervals for the two design choices, equi-spaced points and
D-optimal points for the ninth degree polynomial, are shown in Figure 2.9. Not surprisingly,
the predictions are similar to those of kernel ridge regression, but the advantage of the
Gaussian process regression is that we are able to provide uncertainty intervals for the
predictions. Compared to the eighth degree polynomial given in the right panels of Figures
2.6 and 2.7, the predictions as well as the uncertainty intervals of the Gaussian process
regression are much better. The uncertainty intervals of Gaussian process regression make
more sense because they are narrower around the regions where we observed the data and
wider in data-sparse regions.

3
Model-based Designs
As we know how to approximate the response surface for given data, we will turn our
attention to how to optimally collect this data. As discussed in Chapter 1, we will use a two-
stage strategy for experimental design: first an initial design and then a sequential design.
In the initial design stage, we will not make any assumptions about the underlying input-
output relationship, and we will try to collect the data to obtain a good approximation
of the entire response surface. In the sequential design stage, we will continue the data
collection process using the model estimated from the initial data in order to satisfy certain
objectives of the experiment, such as optimization. We will focus on initial designs in this
chapter and the next three chapters and discuss sequential designs in Chapter 7. The case
of experimental designs for noisy data will be developed later in the chapter on physical
experiments.
3.1 Prediction-based Designs
Suppose that we have two inputs (x 1 and x2) and a budget to do seven experiments. Let
X be the experimental region from which we need to choose these seven points. Unless
otherwise mentioned, we assume that the experimental region can be scaled into a hy-
percube. So, here X = [0, 1]2. If we do not use any experimental design ideas, then we
could randomly sample seven points from [0, 1]2. Three such random designs are shown in
Figure 3.1. We can see that the first design places the points mostly on the left side of X ,
the second design places the points mostly on the middle side, and the third design places
the points mostly on the right side. Clearly, they are not “good” designs to learn an input-
output relationship in X . It may so happen because they are the best designs for certain
functions. For example, a function that varies more on the left side and almost constant on
the right side can probably be well approximated using the first design. However, in the ex-
perimental design stage, we do not know anything about the function. Therefore, we should
design the experiment in such a way that any possible function can be well approximated
using the data. Therefore, we need to consider a class of functions and look for designs that
will work well on average or minimize worst-case scenarios. For this purpose, we will use the
stochastic framework provided by kriging or GP. The earlier developments using related
ideas can be found in Sacks and Ylvisaker (1970) and Ylvisaker (1975). See Sacks et al.
(1989) for a historical account. We will soon see that these model-based optimal designs
cannot be used in practice because of their dependence on the unknown model parameters.
However, the model-based approach will help us develop optimal design criteria that will
guide us in developing more practical designs in the next chapter.
Consider a GP model for the response surface: h(x) ∼ GP (µ, τ2R(·)). Assume for
the moment that µ, τ 2, and the correlation parameters θ are known. Given the data

--- Page 55 ---
42 Model-based Designs

because the objective function contains R−1, requires numerical integration, and is multi-
modal. We have used the derivative-free limited-memory BFGS optimization algorithm in
the R function optim to find the optimal design by initializing the optimization using the
equi-spaced design. The optimal design (D 3) is shown in the bottom left panel of the same
figure, which givesIM SE(D3; 0.1) = 0.0496. We can see that this design pulled the points to
the center, which made the RMSE at the boundaries large, but overall it has a smaller IMSE.

The IMSE and MMSE designs can be seen as the Gaussian process analogs of the I-optimal
(2.37) and G-optimal designs (2.36) for linear regression models.
The optimal design (D 4) obtained by minimizing MMSE is shown on the bottom right
panel of Figure 3.2. We can see that this design minimizes the largest uncertainty, which
is obtained by slightly pushing the points toward the boundaries compared to the IMSE
design. The M M SEvalues of all four designs are also shown in Figure 3.2.
All of the foregoing computations were performed assuming a Gaussian correlation func-
tion with θ = 0.1. In reality, we will not be able to guess the length-scale parameter before
doing experiments. Therefore, it is important to find out if this parameter has an effect on
optimal designs. The IMSE and MMSE values are plotted in the top panels of Figure 3.3.
We can see that they change a lot with respect to θ. When θ is small or large, all designs
perform similarly. This makes sense because small θ implies a rough surface, where all the
10-run designs perform poorly in approximating the response surface. Similarly, when θ is
high, we have a smooth response surface and all designs do a good job of approximating
it. It is in the middle region of θ, they behave differently. As in Sacks et al. (1989), we can
define the relative efficiency to better discern the designs:
REI (Di; θ) = mini IM SE(Di; θ)
IM SE(Di; θ) ,
REM (Di; θ) = mini M M SE(Di; θ)
M M SE(Di; θ) ,
for i = 1, 2, 3, 4.
The relative efficiencies of the four designs with respect to IMSE and MMSE are plotted
in the bottom panels of Figure 3.3. We can see that different designs become optimal
depending on the value of θ. For example, the Chebyshev design performs poorly with
respect to IMSE when θ < 0.3, but is the best when θ > 0.3. This is bad news for our
optimal designs! However, there is some hope. The equi-spaced design and the MMSE-
optimal design perform reasonably well for all θ even if they are not the best. Thus, we
need to find designs that are robust to θ, which can be formulated as
max

IM SE(D; θ) ,
where

--- Page 56 ---
Maximum Entropy Designs 43
FIGURE 3.3: Relative efficiency of the four designs in Figure 3.2 with respect to IMSE and
MMSE. The IMSE and MMSE optimal designs are found by fixing θ = 0.1.
and Θ ⊂ [0, ∞)p. Robust MMSE-optimal designs can be defined similarly. This is an ex-
tremely difficult problem to solve, as we need to find IMSE- or MMSE-optimal designs for
many values of θ even if we discretize the p-dimensional space Θ.
As can be seen in Figure 3.3, all the designs perform poorly when θ → 0, that is, when
the correlations go to 0. This is the case of near independence. For this case, R → I, the
identity matrix, and therefore, there is no need to invert theR matrix to compute the IMSE
or MMSE criteria. This substantially simplifies the computational burden. Furthermore, the
designs found in this limiting case seem to work reasonably well for large correlations as well.
Thus, these designs, called space-filling designs, are much easier to construct and seem to be
robust to misspecifications of the correlation structure. Therefore, we will use space-filling
designs as initial designs in computer experiments. However, before delving into space-filling
designs, we need to learn about one more important concept in model-based designs.
3.2 Maximum Entropy Designs
Maximum entropy designs were proposed by Shewry and Wynn (1987) for spatial sampling,
which is introduced to the field of computer experiments by Currin et al. (1991). Entropy is

--- Page 57 ---
44 Model-based Designs
a measure of uncertainty (Shannon, 1948). Consider a discrete set X = {X1, . . . ,XN } and
denote the random vector (Y1, . . . , YN ) by YX , where Yi = h(Xi) for some random function
h(·). Then, the entropy of YX is defined as
Ent(YX ) = −
Z
f (y) logf (y) dy,
where f (y) is the probability density function of YX . The objective is to choose a design
D, which is a subset of X . The design D gives the data YD. We aim to choose a D such
that it will minimize the entropy of the unobserved data: YX \D given the observed data
YD. The entropy has the following decomposition (Cover and Thomas, 1991):
Ent(YX ) = Ent(YD) + E{Ent(YX \D|YD)}.
Since the left side is independent of D, the second term on the right side can be minimized
by maximizing Ent(YD) with respect to D. This gives a maximum entropy design.
Assume that the input-output relationship is a realization of a GP: h(x) ∼
GP (µ, τ2R(·)), where µ,τ 2, and the correlation parameters θ are known. Let D = {xi}n
i=1
be the design and y = (y1, . . . , yn)′ the data. Then
y ∼ Nn(µ1, τ2R),
where R = {R(xi − xj)}n
i,j=1. Our aim is to choose D to maximize the entropy of y. The
entropy of y can be computed as:
Ent(y) = −
Z 

A 10-run entropy design is obtained for the one-dimensional problem with R(x) =

RMSE profile. The optimal IMSE and MMSE designs in Figure 3.2 are also shown here
for quick comparison. We can see that the entropy design points are pushed toward the
boundaries compared to the IMSE and MMSE optimal designs. The maximum entropy
design is close to the equi-spaced design in Figure 3.2, but has a smaller maximum RMSE.
Maximum entropy designs have the same practical limitations as the IMSE and MMSE
designs mentioned in the previous section. First, they can be computed only after specifying
the correlation parameters, which are unknown at the design stage. Second, although simpler

than the IMSE/MMSE criteria, computing the determinant ofR is still expensive because it
requires O(n3) operations, and therefore the optimization of entropy can be time consuming.
Furthermore, the objective function can be multi-modal, making the optimization even
harder. We have used local optimization algorithms with multiple starts or a good initial
start to find the optimal design inFigure 3.4, but it may not be efficient in higher dimensions.
Currin et al. (1991) used the DETMAX algorithm of Mitchell (1974). Advanced optimization
algorithms for discrete problems can be found in Fampa and Lee (2022).
We will overcome the robustness and optimization issues of maximum entropy designs
by finding them in low-correlation regimes (rough surfaces), which will lead to a type of
space-filling designs discussed in the next chapter.

--- Page 60 ---

Space-filling Designs
Although the model-based approach gives us a principled way to construct optimal designs,
it has certain practical limitations. The model-based optimal designs depend on the un-
known model parameters such as the correlations parameters. Unfortunately, they are not
robust to the misspecification of those parameters. Moreover, the model-based criteria are
computationally expensive and numerically unstable, making their optimization difficult
and computationally expensive. Therefore, in this chapter, we will consider approximations
of the model-based criteria and develop designs that aim to fill the experimental region.
They have nice geometric interpretations and can be motivated by different types of mod-
eling method, which indicates that they possess some sort of model robustness.

The L2-quantization problem is easier to solve mathematically and was proposed in commu-
nications theory by Lloyd (1957) and Max (1960). It is also related to the idea of optimum
stratified sampling (Dalenius, 1950; Cox, 1957) and representative points of a distribution
(Flury, 1990; Fang and Wang, 1993). Eq. (4.7) is the most popular criterion used in clus-
ter analysis and can be solved using Lloyd’s kmeans algorithm, which alternates between
finding the Voronoi regions and their means. However, the problem of minimizing the sum
of distances in (4.5) seems to have appeared much earlier in facility location analysis and
computational geometry (Weber, 1929). An algorithm similar to k-means that uses itera-
tive least squares for computing the centers can be used to solve (4.5), which is known as
Weiszfeld’s algorithm.

fill the space nicely. However, the points seem to be pulled too much toward the center,
which may not be desirable in prediction problems. This is because most statistical and
machine learning models are good for interpolation (predicting inside the convex hull of the
7 points), but extrapolation can be unreliable (predicting outside the convex hull). Lekivetz
and Jones (2015) tried to overcome this issue by choosing a point from each of the Voronoi
regions that is not at its centroid using a secondary criterion such as MaxPro (Joseph et al.,
2015) to be discussed later.

Thus, following (4.9), in order to find a minimax design, we need to first find the farthest
point in each of the Voronoi regions. These are the points that are far away from the design
points, and therefore, it is difficult to make accurate predictions at these points. The worst
point is the farthest one. A minimax design tries to minimize the distance to the worst
point. There could be more than one worst point in the experimental region. The least
number of design points surrounding such a worst point is called the (minimax) index of
the design. We want the index to be large because the prediction at the worst points is very
difficult, but this difficulty reduces if we have more design points around it. Johnson et al.
(1990) proved the following result.
Theorem 4.1 Consider the correlation function R(u, v) = ρk(d(u, v)), where d is a dis-
tance metric, ρ is a decreasing function, and k > 0. Then, a minimax design of highest
index is asymptotically MMSE-optimum as k → ∞.
We can see that the isotropic Gaussian correlation function we have used is a special case
of the correlation function in this theorem. In particular, if we use the Lq distance metric
d(u, v) = ∥u − v∥q, where ∥x∥q = {Pp
i=1 |xi|q}1/q, the minimax design becomes
min
D
max
i=1:n
max
x∈Vi
∥x − xi∥q, (4.11)
Unless otherwise mentioned, we will use Euclidean distance to find the minimax design.
To give an example, suppose that a petroleum company is planning to start some gas
stations in a certain region. A minimax design would suggest placing the gas stations so
that the distance of the farthest customer to his/her closest station is minimized. Thus, a
minimax design will ensure that no customer is too far from one of company’s gas stations.
A minimax design of highest index would imply that the farthest customer is given the
greatest possible choice in alternative gas stations.
It is easy to find minimax designs when p = 1. For example, for n = 2, the minimax
design in X = [0 , 1] is {.25, .75}. The worst points are x = 0 , .5, and 1. All of them are

--- Page 64 ---
Minimax Designs 51
.25 away from the two design points, which is the minimum of the worst distances of any
possible design. More generally, an n-point minimax design is given by

In general, finding minimax designs for p ≥ 2 is very difficult (but much easier than
MMSE designs). Figure 4.2 shows the minimax design for n = 7 and p = 2. Compared to
the clustering-based designs in Figure 4.1, the points are pushed toward the boundaries.
Balls of radius ϕ(D) (the fill distance) are also plotted around each design point. We can
see that these balls cover the entire region X . In fact, a minimax design gives identical balls
with a minimum radius to cover the entire experimental region. Therefore, the minimax
design is also known as a covering design.

minimax design

FIGURE 4.2: A 7-point minimax design.
Minimax designs overcome some of the practical limitations of model-based optimal
designs. First, they do not need the specification of the length-scale parameter (θ). Theorem
4.1 indicates that they are close to optimal when θ ≈ 0 because θ is inversely proportional to
k for Gaussian correlation function. The example in Figure 3.3 shows that designs generated
using small values ofθ are more likely to be robust compared to designs generated using large
θ. Moreover, minimax designs can be thought of as optimal designs for modeling methods
unrelated to GP such as a nearest-neighbor predictor (1-NN), because the predictive ability
of a 1-NN predictor depends on how close the design point is to the prediction point. In
fact, minimax designs can be motivated using purely geometric considerations (designs that
minimize the gaps in the region) without using any modeling methods, which again points
to its robustness.
Second, minimax designs are easier to generate than their counterpart MMSE-optimal
designs because we do not need to compute R−1, which can be computationally expensive
and numerically unstable for large n. Nevertheless, construction of minimax designs is still
expensive for large n and p. Tan (2013) proposed a binary integer programming method to
find minimax designs from a given set of candidate points. However, it becomes computa-
tionally intractable as the number of candidate points increases. Mak and Joseph (2018a)

--- Page 65 ---
52 Space-filling Designs
proposed a clustering-based method to generate minimax designs. First, generate a large
uniform sample {Xj}N

where δij = 1 if Xj ∈ Vi and 0 otherwise. This shows that we can obtain the minimax
design using Lloyd’s k-means type algorithm by setting q to be a large value (but not
too large to cause numerical instability). Since Lloyd’s algorithm is likely to converge to a
local optimum, they further improve the design using particle swarm optimization. Their
implementation of the algorithm is available in the R package minimaxdesign (Mak, 2021).
A more efficient method to construct minimax designs using a special class of lattice designs
is proposed in He (2017a), but it works only for small p. See the R package LatticeDesign.

is called the separation distance (or packing radius) of the design D (Fasshauer, 2007, p.
136). A design that maximizes the separation distance is called a maximin distance design
or simply a maximin design (Johnson et al., 1990), that is
D∗ = argmax
D
min
i̸=j
∥xi − xj∥. (4.16)
Thus, a maximin design tries to maximize the minimum pairwise distance among all the
design points. Such a design minimizes the maximum off-diagonal correlation of R, which
approximately maximizes the entropy of the design. From a geometric point of view, by
separating the points as much as possible, the hope is that a maximin design will fill the
entire experimental region X . Going back to the gas station example that we saw in the
previous section, a maximin design ensures that no two gas stations are too close to each
other. It minimizes the competition from each other by locating the stations as far apart
as possible. Thus, a maximin design is more supplier-focused, whereas a minimax design is
more customer-focused.
A maximin design need not be unique. Figure 4.3 shows two designs with the same
separation distance, but clearly the first design is better because it has only a single pair of
points with the minimum distance, while the second design has two pairs. Call the number
of pairs of points at distance 2ψ (D) as the (maximin) index of the design. We want this
index to be as small as possible because we do not want too many points to be close to
each other. Thus, we can use the index of the design as a secondary criterion to refine the
maximin designs. Johnson et al. (1990) proved the following result.
Theorem 4.2 Consider the correlation function R(u, v) = ρk(d(u, v)), where d is a dis-
tance metric, ρ is a decreasing function, and k >0. Then, a maximin design of lowest index
is asymptotically a maximum entropy design as k → ∞.
Now consider the rational quadratic (RQ) correlation function (Rasmussen and Williams,
2006, p. 86) in Table 2.1, also known as inverse quadratic or Cauchy kernel (Fasshauer and

McCourt, 2015, p. 42):

in O(n log n) as opposed to O(n2) for T RD(D). The special case of T RD(D) with α = 1 is a
criterion proposed by Audze and Eglais (1977), which has a physical analogy of minimizing
the gravitational force among particles of equal mass placed inside a box.

are needed to find the global optimum. To find the maximin design in (4.16), we can use a
derivative-free global optimization, which can be slow for large n and p. This algorithm is
implemented in the R package SFDesign (Wang and Joseph, 2025c).
The maximin design for n = 7 and p = 2 obtained using SFDesign is shown in Figure 4.4,
which has a separation distance of ψ(D∗) = 0.26795. Circles are drawn around each point
with radius ψ(D∗). A maximin design maximizes the radius of identical non-overlapping

balls that can be placed around each design point, which makes this problem related to the
well-known sphere packing problem in mathematics (Conway and Sloane, 2013). However,
most of the theoretical results on sphere packing are about arranging spheres in Rp as
opposed to a hypercube in our case. The boundary constraints make the maximin designs
different from the optimal sphere-packing configurations, but there has been some effort
to use the results of the sphere-packing to solve the maximin design problem (He, 2017b).
These are difficult mathematical problems, although the solutions for some specific cases are
trivial. As Borodachov et al. (2019, p.11) put it “The proof of best-packing configurations
in R3 was an open problem for nearly four centuries, but anyone who has ever stacked
oranges already knew the answer”. See the website http://www.packomania.com/ for the
best sphere-packing results on squares and cubes, and the references therein.
An advantage of maximin designs over cluster-based and minimax designs is that it can
be easily adapted for sequential construction. For example, we can start with a point x1,
and then sequentially add x2, . . . ,xn by
xk+1 = argmax
x∈X

This method was originally proposed by Kennard and Stone (1969) for physical experiments.
Several variants and improvements to this algorithm are proposed in Shang and Apley
(2021). Note that these are sequential construction methods for initial designs, whereas the
sequential design methods to be discussed in Chapter 7 are done after acquiring all the
data, including the responses.
The left panel of Figure 4.5 shows six points sequentially added to the center point using
the maximin criterion. This can be done using the maximin.augment function in SFDesign
(Wang and Joseph, 2025c). The separation distance of the resulting design is ψ(D) = 0.25,
which is smaller than that of the optimal maximin design in Figure 4.4. Six more points are
added to this design sequentially and we obtain the design in the right plot of Figure 4.5,
which looks like a good design (but not the optimal 13-point maximin design).
