uncertainties.
Suppose that we have a budget of eight runs. Then, the locally D-optimal design with
initial guess η0 = (0, 2, 2)′ will have four points each replicated twice, as shown in the left
panel of Figure 9.2. Now suppose that the following distributions capture our uncertainty
of the parameters: η1 ∼ U(1, 3) and η2 ∼ N(2, 1/22). We can obtain N samples from their
uncertainty distributions and obtain the corresponding locally D-optimal designs. Since
finding a locally D-optimal design in general can be computationally cumbersome, we can
use support points in (5.11) to obtain a small number of good representative samples to
reduce computational burden. The N locally D-optimal design points are shown as black
circles in the right panel of Figure 9.2. We can see that they vary quite a lot, showing that
the locally D-optimal design generated with η0 = (0, 2, 2)′ can be poor.
How can we find optimal designs robust to the misspecifications of the parameters?
Dror and Steinberg (2006) proposed a simple idea to find the robust design by clustering
the 4N points using kmeans. In this case, all the points have equal weight and therefore the
clustering would work. However, in general, each point in the locally D-optimal design can
have a different weight. Moreover, clustering can distort the underlying distribution and
may not produce good representative points of the distribution. Therefore, Krishna et al.
(2022) proposed to use the importance support points (Huang et al., 2022) in (5.17) to find
the robust design with weights adjusted using w ← w/N. The eight points thus obtained
are shown in the right panel of Figure 9.2, which can be considered robust to parameter
uncertainties.
9.2.2 Model Uncertainties
As George Box famously said “All models are wrong, but some are useful”, it is very likely
that the model h(x; η∗) is not the true model µ(x). This is because several assumptions

--- Page 190 ---
Robust Designs 177
are made to simplify the modeling and make it mathematically tractable. Some of these
assumptions may not hold true in practice and therefore, the resulting model may not agree
with the reality even with the best possible choice of the calibration parameters. Take, for
example, the vapor phase infiltration process. The measured mass uptake at 8.7 torr vapor
pressure and 130 oC temperature on a 483 nm thick polymer film shown in Figure 1.2 is
reproduced in Figure 9.3. The prediction from the reaction-diffusion model with parameters
estimated using the Bayesian functional optimization method of Huang et al. (2021) is also
plotted in the same figure. Although the predictions generally agree with the measurements,
significant departure can also be observed, especially during the initial part of the sorption.
0 50 100 150 200 250 300 350
5000 15000 25000 35000
model prediction vs real data
t
mass uptake
measured
predicted
FIGURE 9.3: The measured mass uptake from the physical experiment and the calibrated
reaction-diffusion model prediction.
Ren et al. (2021) point out several possible reasons for the model discrepancy. A key
assumption used for deriving the reaction-diffusion model is that the swelling and relaxation
of the polymer can be ignored. However,in situ spectroscopic ellipsometry studies performed
clearly show thickness variations during the infiltration process. Nevertheless, this effect is
not easy to model mathematically and thus is ignored. Another modeling assumption is
that the heat generated during the sorption can be ignored, which can again be violated in
practice.
One can investigate the possible reasons for the model discrepancy, fix the wrong as-
sumptions, and try to develop a better physics-based model. This is the usual engineering
approach, but it can be too time-consuming. A simpler option is to fit a statistical model to
the observed discrepancy. Kennedy and O’Hagan (2001) proposed to model the discrepancy
using a Gaussian process:
µ(x) = h(x; η∗) + δ(x), δ(x) ∼ GP (0, τ2R(·)).
Thus, the data from the physical experiment can be modeled using
y = h(x; η) + δ(x) + ϵ, δ(x) ∼ GP (0, τ2R(·)), ϵ
iid
∼ N(0, σ2). (9.15)
Using this model, the calibration parameters and the discrepancy can be estimated simulta-
neously from the data. Note that this model contains two sources of uncertainty in the data:

--- Page 191 ---
178 Model Calibration
δ(x) captures the uncertainties introduced due to the missing physics and ϵ captures the
unknown random effects. In the uncertainty quantification literature (Der Kiureghian and
Ditlevsen, 2009), the former is called epistemic and the latter aleatoric. Epistemic uncer-
tainty can be reduced by adding more physics into the model or collecting more data, where
as aleatoric uncertainties are irreducible unless we can find the sources of the randomness
(Joseph et al., 2024).
Although the predictions from the Kennedy-O’Hagan model are good, Tuo and Wu
(2015, 2016) showed that the estimate of calibration parameters can be poor due to the non-
identifiability between η and δ(x). Plumlee (2017) proposed to use an orthogonal Gaussian
process (Plumlee and Joseph, 2018) to overcome the identifiability issue. Other approaches
to deal with the identifiability issue in model calibration are proposed in Gu and Wang
(2018), Tuo (2019), and Zhang and Wang (2025). See Sung and Tuo (2024) for a recent
review. A much simpler approach is to use a two-stage procedure for model fitting: first,
estimate the calibration parameters using least squares, and then fit a Gaussian process to
the residuals (Joseph and Melkote, 2009; Wong et al., 2017). To make this approach clear,
first consider the case of analytical models. Then, the least squares estimate of η can be
obtained as in (9.7). The residuals are given by
ei = yi − h(xi; ˆη), i= 1, . . . , n.
We can now fit a Gaussian process regression with zero mean to the data {(xi, ei)}n
i=1 as
described in Section 2.2.3, which gives
ˆτ 2 = 1
n e′(R + λI)−1e,
(ˆλ, ˆθ) = argmin
λ,θ

n log ˆτ 2 + log |R + λI|
	
,
where λ = σ2/τ 2. Then, the prediction at a new location x is given by
µ(x)|y ∼ N(ˆy(x), s2(x)),
where
ˆy(x) = h(x; ˆη) + r(x)′(R + ˆλI)−1(y − h(ˆη))
s2(x) = ˆτ 2
n
1 + ˆλ − r(x)′(R + ˆλI)−1r(x)
o
.
Although simple, this approach ignores the uncertainties in the estimation of η. However,
it is a less serious issue than obtaining a wrong estimate of η with a nonidentifiable model.
If h is a computationally expensive model, we can first obtain a surrogate model bh(x; η)
and proceed as before with the two-stage procedure. However, such an approach will ignore
the uncertainties in the surrogate model predictions. Kennedy and O’Hagan (2001) explains
how to incorporate them in the final prediction using a Gaussian process for h. Chang and
Joseph (2014) and Joseph and Yan (2015) propose an approximate way to account for these
uncertainties which can be seamlessly integrated into the two-stage procedure.
Let us return to the experimental design problem. Motivated by the two-stage proce-
dure, Krishna et al. (2022) proposed a simple idea to find robust designs against model
uncertainties. In the previous section, we have already learned about finding robust designs
for estimating calibration parameters. Denote this design by Dη. In Chapter 4, we learned
about space-filling designs for fitting a GP model, which are robust to misspecifications of
the correlation parameters. Suppose that we have a budget of n runs, of which we have used
n1 runs in Dη. Then we can find a space-filling design in n − n1 runs to augment the design
Dη. We will illustrate this approach using an example.

--- Page 192 ---
Robust Designs 179
Consider again the two-factor exponential model in (9.12). Suppose that we have a bud-
get of eight runs. Figure 9.4 shows four runs (solid blue circles) obtained using importance
support points from locally D-optimal designs generated using samples from η1 ∼ U(1, 3)
and η2 ∼ N(2, 1/22). The remaining four runs can be obtained using sequential MaxPro
in (4.27), which can be easily generated using the MaxProAugment function in the MaxPro
package (Ba and Joseph, 2018). They are shown as red crosses in Figure 9.4. This design
can be considered robust to both parameter and model uncertainties.

Sequential design is an attractive option in model calibration. See Williams et al. (2011),

Part IV
Data Science

--- Page 197 ---


--- Page 198 ---
10
Data Subsampling
So far we have been looking at the problem of where to collect the data. In this chapter,
we will look at a different problem–the data have already been collected, but they are too
big for storage or analysis and, therefore, we need to choose a subset of the data. We want
to choose the subset in such a way that we lose very little information that is contained in
the big data. This is very similar to the experimental design problem. However, there are
two main differences. First, in the experimental design problem, the experimental space is
generally continuous, whereas in the subset data selection problem, the space is discrete
because the big data restrict our choices to a finite number of cases. Second, the response
is not available while designing the experiment, but in the subset data selection problem,
we already have the responses.
10.1 Support Points-based Subsampling
Since the data we have in hand is a sample from the population, the subset of the data can
also be viewed as a subsample. A common technique to obtain a subsample is to choose
some of the rows of the data matrix uniformly at random. However, as we have observed
earlier in the experimental design problem, the random sample can sometimes be good and
sometimes very poor. We need to use a better sampling technique to get more consistent and
robust results. For fitting a linear regression model, Wang et al. (2019) proposed choosing
samples by maximizing the D-optimality criterion. However, such a subsample is optimal
only for the assumed linear model. If we need to fit a different model, say a random forest
or a Gaussian process model, then the chosen subsample will no longer be optimal. There
is a growing literature on coresets (or core-sets) in machine learning (Feldman, 2019) which
again seems to focus on a specific parametric model. Since statistical modeling is an iterative
process, it is imperative to choose a subsample without relying on a specific model.
One possible approach to do this is to choose a subsample which has the same distribu-
tion as the original dataset. Then, all the properties that we compute using the subsample
will approximately match with those of the dataset. If we can optimally match the distribu-
tion, then we will lose very little information in using the subsample. Thus, we can use the
techniques described in Chapter 5 to obtain the best possible representative sample (Chen
et al., 2012; Mak and Joseph, 2018c,b; Shang et al., 2023).
Let D = {Zi = (X i, Yi)}N
i=1 be the dataset, where there are p input variables and q
output variables. Our aim is to obtain a subsample S = {zi = (xi, yi)}n
i=1, where n < Nso
that the empirical distribution of the subsample is as close to the empirical distribution of
the dataset. First, standardize all variables to have unit variance so that Euclidean distance
makes sense. Then, the distance between these two empirical distributions can be measured
DOI: 10.1201/9781003661535-10 185
This chapter has been made available under a CC-BY-NC-ND license 4.0.

--- Page 199 ---
186 Data Subsampling
using the energy distance (Sz´ ekely et al., 2004):
ED(S, D) = 2

The major difference between (5.13) and (10.1) is that the former performs a continuous
optimization, whereas the latter performs a discrete optimization. Therefore, we cannot use
the difference-of-convex programming technique that we used for solving (5.13). However,
discrete optimization is generally a much more difficult problem. Our main motivation to
obtain a subsample was to save the computational burden of handling the big dataset. There-
fore, if it is time-consuming to obtain the subsample, then the whole purpose is defeated.
One approach to circumvent this issue is to first use the difference-of-convex programming
to quickly find the support points in the continuous space spanned by the dataset. Then,
we can use a nearest neighbor algorithm to choose the closest point in the dataset. How-
ever, a naive nearest neighbor assignment can lead to duplicates. Therefore, Joseph and
Vakayil (2022) proposed a one-at-a-time sequential nearest-neighbor algorithm, where a
point is removed from the dataset if it is already selected. This is implemented in the R
function subsample in the R package SPlit (Vakayil et al., 2022), which uses kd-tree for
fast nearest-neighbor assignment.

Support Points-based Subsampling 187
nearest-neighbor assignment may cause a slight distortion to the distribution, which can be
neglected.
Now consider the problem of predicting power from wind turbines based on wind speed
(Ding, 2019). The dataset1 contains 39,195 data collected in 10-minute time intervals. They
are plotted in Figure 10.2. Suppose that we are interested in fitting a Gaussian process
model to this data. However, this data size is too large for a GP. Therefore, we sampled 1%
of the data using the support points-based subsampling method and fitted a GP (ordinary
kriging) with a Gaussian correlation function using rkriging (Huang and Joseph, 2024).
The data reduction took about two seconds and the model fitting on 392 data points took
less than one second. Thus, the overall fitting could be done in less than three seconds.
This is a great saving on computational time because fitting on the original dataset would
have taken several days. The predictions are shown in Figure 10.2. Unfortunately, they do
not look good for low and high speeds, which is a well-known issue with stationary GPs.
The constant mean assumption of a stationary GP causes a “mean reversion”, that is,
predictions will go back to the prior mean in data-sparse regions (Joseph, 2006b). We will
revisit this problem later in Chapter 11 and explain how the GP can be modified to improve
the predictions with large datasets.
FIGURE 10.2: The plot shows 392 subsamples taken from the wind power dataset of size
39,195 and the GP model fitted using the subsample.

10.2 Data Splitting
Suppose that we have several models that explain the data well. Then, which model should
we deploy in the field? The best way to answer this question is to deploy all the models and
then choose the one that performs well, but this is almost impossible to do in practice. A
common way to circumvent this problem is to split the data into two parts: training and
testing. Then, use only the training data to estimate the models and use the testing data
to choose the best model. Under the assumption that the distribution of the data remains
the same when we deploy the models to the field (i.e., there is no covariate shift), we should
obtain the testing data that have the same distribution as the entire dataset. Randomly
sampling some rows from the data matrix is one way to do this. For example, if we are
interested in splitting the data in an 80:20 ratio, then we can randomly sample 20% of the
rows to obtain the testing set and the remaining 80% will become the training set. However,
random sampling causes too much variability in the test results, making the choice of model
unreliable.
Clearly, we could use ideas from space-filling designs to select a good testing set that
uniformly covers the entire dataset. Interestingly, one of the earliest applications of space-
filling designs was to choose a testing set from a given dataset. Snee (1977) proposed a
modification of the sequential maximin designs in (4.20) (Kennard and Stone, 1969) to
find an optimal testing set. As we have observed earlier, the maximin design pushes the
points to the boundaries, and therefore the testing set would have a distribution that is
much more dispersed than the original dataset. Snee’s idea was to alternate the one-point-
at-a-time greedy selection of the points in (4.20) between the training and testing sets.
This algorithm, known as DUPLEX, produces a much nicer testing set. However, as shown
in Joseph and Vakayil (2022), the distribution of the DUPLEX testing set can still be
very different from that of the original dataset. To maintain the distribution and obtain a
well-distributed testing set, Joseph and Vakayil (2022) proposed to use the support points-
based subsampling algorithm discussed in the previous section. They termed this algorithm
as SPlit. Although the algorithm is the same as the one used for data subsampling, the
motivation here is completely different, and it can also be applied to small datasets.
For illustration, consider a synthetic dataset of 100 points from Joseph and Vakayil
(2022). There are two input variables ( x1 and x2) and one categorical output variable y
that has three levels. The data is plotted in Figure 10.3 with the three categories shown in
red-triangles, green-pluses, and blue-crosses. Suppose that we are interested in splitting this
dataset in an 80:20 ratio. The SPlit function in the R package SPlit (Vakayil et al., 2022)
automatically codes the categorical variable using Helmert coding mentioned in Section 8.3,
computes the support points using the difference-of-convex program, and selects the sub-
sample using the sequential nearest neighbor algorithm. The 20 testing points thus obtained
are shown in the right panel of Figure 10.3. The left panel shows a random testing set. We
can see that the SPlit testing set is much more balanced and representative of the original
dataset than that of the random testing set. This will help compute the generalization error
more accurately and will help select the best model in a more consistent way.

10.3 Data Twinning
The computational complexity of finding n support points from N data points is O(pn(n +
N )m/P ), where m is the number of iterations used in the difference-of-convex program and
P the number of processor cores available for parallel computation. The average complexity
of building a kd-tree is O(pN log N ). Thus, the average complexity of support points-based
subsampling or splitting is O(pN log N + pn(n + N )m/P ). Let γ = n/N be the splitting
ratio, which is usually in (0, 0.5] (Joseph, 2022). Therefore, the overall complexity can be
simplified to
O

pN log N + mpγN 2
P

.
This grows quadratic in N and therefore SPlit cannot be used for large datasets. Vakayil
and Joseph (2022a) proposed an algorithm to overcome this computational bottleneck,
which is described next.
So far we have been discussing about choosing a small subset S of size n from a large
dataset D of size N . The complement of S is the dataset S which is of size N − n. The
energy distance between the empirical distributions of these two sets can be calculated as
ED(S, S) = 2

--- Page 203 ---
190 Data Subsampling
Therefore, minimization of ED(S, S) and ED(S, D) will produce identical results. Never-
theless, the idea of twinning provides a new perspective of the optimization problem, which
can be used to develop a heuristic algorithm to solve the problem.
First, note that ED(S, S) contains three terms. Therefore, minimization of ED(S, S)
can be achieved by (i) bringing S and S closer, (ii) spreading S, and (iii) spreading S.
We will explain how these conflicting objectives can be achieved using a simple example.
Consider the 50 data points in Figure 10.4. Suppose that we want to split this dataset into
40 training points and 10 testing points. Start with the point marked “1” as the first testing
point. Choose the nearest four points to it and assign them to the training set. This is to
bring S and
S closer together. Now find the farthest point from “1” among the four points
and choose the closest point to it as “2”, which will be the second testing point. This ensures
spreading of
S. This algorithm can be continued until we obtain 10 points in the testing
set, which is shown in the right panel of the same figure. All of these computations can be
done using a kd-tree with an average computational complexity of O(pN log N ). This is a
huge saving over the SPlit algorithm. However, as seen in the right panel, the convex hull
of points starts to overlap in later iterations, making the selection of testing points not so
good. Nevertheless, Vakayil and Joseph (2022a) have shown that the twinning algorithm
produces testing sets as good as the SPlit algorithm or even better sets when N is large.

Consider again the wind power dataset that contains 39,195 data points. The twinning
algorithm is implemented in the R package twinning (Vakayil and Joseph, 2022b) and in
a Python package2. Choosing 392 points from the 39,195 points using twinning took only
about 0.02 seconds, which is 100 times faster than the SPlit algorithm.
Because twinning is so fast that it can be efficiently applied to other data science
applications such as partitioning the dataset into multiple sets for cross-validation or divide-
and-conquer procedures. See Joseph et al. (2024) for an application of twinning to speed-up
a computationally expensive cross-validation-based estimation procedure.
In survey sampling, for populations with spatial trends, it is of interest to select samples
that are well spread over the population. A well-spread sample is known as spatially bal-
anced, which is synonymous with space-filling designs in the experimental design literature.
There are many methods available in the survey sampling literature to obtain a spatially
balanced sample. See the book by Till´ e (2006) for details. To make a comparison with these
methods, consider a simulated dataset Xi

10.4 Supervised Compression
Although the datasets include response information, we have not explicitly used them to
select the subsample, except to ensure that the join distribution of the subsample S =
{(xi, yi)}n
i=1 matches the join distribution of the dataset D = {(Xi, Yi)}n
i=1. However, if
the purpose of obtaining the subsample is to develop a predictive model, then it makes
sense to choose samples based on the information in the responses. For example, consider
the wind power data in Figure 10.2. Since power has little variation for speeds less than 3

--- Page 205 ---
192 Data Subsampling
m/s or more than 13 m/s, it might be enough to get the subsample for speed in the range
[3, 13]. This is done under the assumption that our modeling method is capable of modeling
this non-stationary behavior of the input-output relationship. As shown in Figure 10.2, the
stationary Gaussian process was clearly unable to do a good job with this dataset. We might
need to use Gaussian processes that can handle non-stationarity such as the Treed Gaussian
process (Gramacy and Lee, 2008) or the composite Gaussian process (Ba and Joseph, 2012).
Suppose that we have a continuous scalar output. Denote the fitted model using the
subsample by y = ˆh(x|S). Assuming a squared-error loss, our objective is to find the optimal
subsample using

We need to make a choice of ˆh before this optimization can be performed. If we choose it
to be a linear regression model, then the optimal subsample will be optimal only for that
chosen linear model and may not work well with the other models. Therefore, to make the
subsample robust to the modeling choices, we need to choose a nonparametric regression
model for ˆh. However, if we choose a treed or composite Gaussian process, then its estimation
and computation will be expensive, and therefore the time to find an optimal subsample
will be too high for it to be of any use. Thus, we need to choose a simple non-parametric
model that is flexible and fast to evaluate. Joseph and Mak (2021) proposed to use a nearest
neighbor method (1-NN) for this purpose.
The nearest neighbor predictor is given by

comparison, the unsupervised selection of points using kmeans is shown in the left panel
of the same figure. We can see that kmeans uniformly spreads out the points in the input
region, whereas the supercompress algorithm chooses more points in the region where the
response has steep changes and fewer points in the flat regions. As a result, Voronoi regions
with kmeans are roughly of the same size, while those with supercompress are small in the
region of high variation and large in the region of low variation. Clearly, a model capable of
capturing the non-stationarity of the function will do much better with the points selected
using supercompress than with those of kmeans. Quantitatively, the root mean squared
prediction error of a nearest neighbor predictor is 0.1556 with the unsupervised set of points
and 0.0996 with the supervised set of points, showing a 36% improvement in prediction
accuracy. However, the computational complexity of the supercompress algorithm is of the

Exercises
1. Suppose the dataset D = {Zi}N
i=1 is split into S = {zi}n
i=1 and
S = {vi}N −n
i=1 .
(a) Prove the identity in (10.2).
(b) Show that ED(S, D) + ED(S, D) is minimized when n = N/2.
2. Most statistical and machine learning models have some hyper-parameters or regulariza-
tion parameters, which are commonly estimated from the training set by holding out a
validation set, say of size m. Thus, we want to split the dataset into three sets: training,
validation, and testing. Consider the following two approaches to doing this.
Approach 1: Split the dataset D of size N into two sets S and S of size n and N − n,
respectively using SPlit. Then split S into two sets of size m and N − n − m again using
SPlit. Use the set of size m as the validation test and the other as the training set.
Approach 2: Split the dataset D of size N into two sets S and S of size n + m and
N − n − m, respectively using SPlit. Then split S into two sets of size n and m again
using SPlit. Use the set of size m as the validation test and the other as the testing set.
Which approach creates a better validation set so that the testing performance is not
influenced by the model estimation/validation step?
3. Suppose that we want to split a dataset into four representative sets of 25% points
each. For example, this could be used for a four-fold cross-validation. Vakayil and Joseph
(2022a) describes three strategies to do this as shown inFigure 10.7, which is implemented
in the function multiplet in the R packagetwinning. To assess the performance of these
strategies, randomly generate a dataset of size N = 1, 000 from

(without replacement), LPM1, and LPM2. Compare them using energy distance and
spatial balance measures.

11
Data Analysis
A major goal of data analysis is to identify the important input variables that affect the
output and to build a predictive model using the important variables. Traditionally, experi-
mental design is not viewed as a technique for data analysis but as a tool for data collection
and planning. The purpose of this chapter is to show that the experimental design or its
related techniques that we discussed in earlier chapters can be used for data analysis and
predictive modeling. We will see that a careful integration of these techniques into data anal-
ysis and modeling can yield better and faster results, especially when dealing with big data.
11.1 Factor Selection and Ranking
Variable selection is considered one of the most important topics in statistics, which has
recently seen renewed interest due to the need for interpreting and explaining black-box
statistical and machine learning models. This topic has been in the literature for a long time
and the fact that the research on it has not been settled speaks to its difficulty. Fortunately,
we have already laid the necessary groundwork for developing variable selection techniques
in Chapter 6. We can use the global sensitivity indices for quantifying the importance of
variables and use them for selection and ranking. However, in data analysis problems, we
only have a dataset {(x(i), y(i))}N
i=1, where x(i) ∈ Rp. This means that we do not have
access to the computer model or know the distribution of the variables, which substantially
increases the complexity of the problem.
To understand the challenges associated with measuring factor importance in data anal-
ysis problems, let us first recall some of the concepts discussed in Chapter 6. One of the
main measures that we used for factor screening was the total Sobol’ index (Homma and
Saltelli, 1996). Let y = h(x), x ∈ [0, 1]p be the input-output relationship which we assume
to be known or that at least we have access to a computer code which can be used for
obtaining the output given the input values. By treating x to be a uniformly distributed
random variable, i.e., x ∼ U(0, 1)p, the total Sobol’ index for the ith factor is defined as
Ti = V tot
i
V = E{var[h(x)|x∼i]}
var{h(x)} . (11.1)
We developed Monte Carlo and experimental design-based techniques to estimate them
and then declared a factor to be important if Ti is large and unimportant if Ti is small.
Unfortunately, many things are different in a data analysis problem: (i) we do not know
the input-output relationship, (ii) the joint and conditional distributions of the inputs are
DOI: 10.1201/9781003661535-11 196
This chapter has been made available under a CC-BY-NC-ND license 4.0.

--- Page 210 ---
Factor Selection and Ranking 197
unknown and can be far from uniform, (iii) the data are noisy and can be unstructured
with no option to collect new data using experimental design, and (iv) the input variables
need not be independent.
We can try to overcome the first issue by estimating the unknown input-output relation-
ship from data using nonparametric regression or machine learning techniques. However,
such an approach can be difficult and time-consuming, especially with large amounts of
data. Moreover, errors in estimation can bias the importance measures. Similarly, we can
try to overcome the second issue through density estimation, but it can be an arduous task
by itself, especially in high-dimensional problems. If we are able to solve the first two issues,
we can solve the third issue by using the estimated function and distribution.
Here we will describe another approach to simultaneously take care of the first three
issues. However, the fourth one on dependent inputs is a serious issue because it questions
the validity of Sobol’ indices, which requires some discussions before we proceed with this
approach. If the variables are not independent, then the orthogonality that we used to
decompose the variance does not hold true anymore, and therefore the functional ANOV A
which formed the basis of Sobol’ indices breaks down. As an example, let
y = 2x1 + 2
√
3 x2 + x3, (11.2)
x1
x2

∼ N

0

,
1 ρ

, x3 ∼ N(0, 1).
Since the model is additive, it is easy to visualize the effect of each variable as shown in the
left panel of Figure 11.1. If we ignore the correlations, clearly x1 is the most important vari-
able, x2 the second most important, and x3 the least important. However, if the correlation
|ρ| is high, then adding x2 to the model when x1 is already in the model is not useful and
vice versa. Since x1 is more important than x2, it would make sense to develop a predictive
model using ( x1, x3) and ignore x2.
FIGURE 11.1: The effects of the three variables for the model in (11.2) (left) and their total
Sobol’ indices (right).
Now let us look at the total Sobol’ indices for this simple linear model. Since the denom-
inator in (11.1) is a constant for all the variables, we only need to consider the numerator
V tot
i . They can be obtained as
V tot
1 = 4(1 − ρ2), Vtot

3 (1 − ρ2), and V tot

--- Page 211 ---
198 Data Analysis
and are plotted in the right panel of Figure 11.1. When the correlation is small, specifically
when |ρ| < 0.5, the total Sobol’ index agrees with our intuition that x1 ≻ x2 ≻ x3, where
“≻” means “more important than”. However, when correlation is high, the total Sobol’
index does not seem to make any sense. In fact, as |ρ| →1, the total Sobol index for both
x1 and x2 goes to zero, indicating that both of these variables are unimportant. This is
clearly not the case!
Many proposals exist in the literature that attempt to overcome the deficiency of total
Sobol’ indices with dependent inputs. Among them, a prominent approach is to compute the
importance measure using game-theoretic Shapley values (Owen, 2014; Song et al., 2016),
which can be viewed as a weighted average of total Sobol’ indices computed over all possible
subsets of variables (Verdinelli and Wasserman, 2024). Another interesting approach is the
conditional density-based sensitivity measure proposed by Borgonovo (2007). We refer to
the book by Da Veiga et al. (2021, Ch. 5) and the references therein for details.
Hart and Gremaud (2018) provided a new perspective of the total Sobol’ index that it can
be viewed as the L2 approximation error when a variable is removed from the model keeping
the other variables fixed. To see this, first note that the best model that approximates a
function y = h(x) when a variable xi is removed can be obtained as
h∼i(x∼i) = argmin
g(x∼i)
E{h(x) − g(x∼i)}2 = E{h(x)|x∼i}.
With this optimal choice, the approximation error becomes
E{h(x) − h∼i(x∼i)}2 = E{h(x) − E[h(x)|x∼i]}2
= E
 
E{h(x) − E[h(x)|x∼i]|x∼i}2
= E (var{h(x)|x∼i})
= V tot
i .
Huang and Joseph (2025a) showed that this interpretation of total Sobol’ index agrees with
the leave-out-covariate (LOCO) approach proposed in Lei et al. (2018), Williamson et al.
(2021), and Verdinelli and Wasserman (2024). In fact, they proved the following result.
Theorem 11.1 The total Sobol’ index Ti = 0 if and only if h(x) ⊥ xi | x∼i, that is, h(x)
and xi are independent conditional on x∼i.
Thus, although the absolute value of total Sobol’ index may not make sense in the case of
dependent variables, it can be used as a measure to select factors.
Before the total Sobol’ index can be used for factor selection, we need to develop a
method to estimate it from unstructured, dependent, and noisy data. As mentioned earlier,
we can use a nonparametric regression method to estimate the underlying model from the
data. To reduce the chances of potential bias caused by a poor fitting model, Williamson
et al. (2021) proposed using an ensemble of models. However, this substantially increases
the computational complexity of the approach. To overcome the computational burden and
at the same time achieve model robustness, Huang and Joseph (2025a) proposed a model-
independent approach to the estimation of total Sobol’ indices. Their approach is built on
the nearest neighbor-based estimation procedure of Broto et al. (2020) for clean data.
First note that the numerator of total Sobol’ index can be estimated using a double-
Monte Carlo estimator (Song et al., 2016):

The foregoing estimator can be used to develop a forward selection algorithm as follows.
The variance of h(x) explained by a set of factors u is given by
Vu = var[E{h(x)|xu}]

We can now choose factors one-by-one that maximizes ˆVu. Huang and Joseph (2025a) also
uses a backward elimination to further enhance the selection procedure. The forward and
backward elimination strategies also mitigate the curse of dimensionality with nearest-
neighbor methods. They named the overall algorithm as FIRST, which stands for Factor
Importance Ranking and Selection using Total indices. The algorithm is implemented in the

--- Page 213 ---
200 Data Analysis
R package first (Huang and Joseph, 2025b) and the Python packagepyfirst1. FIRST gave
the best selection performance and was the fastest among the state-of-the-art techniques
for factor selection in the extensive simulations performed by Huang and Joseph (2025a).
The ranking for the selected factors can be done by removing the factor with the smallest
FIRST index one-by-one, and reversing the order of removal. For example, consider the
linear model in (11.2). We generate 1,000 data points with ρ = 0.9. The total Sobol’ indices
can be obtained using the first function in the R package first: ˆT tot
1 = 0.060, ˆT tot
2 =
0
.026, ˆT tot
3 = 0 .114. Thus, x2 is the least important and is removed. Now using only x1 and
x3, we obtain ˆT1 = 0.886 and ˆT tot
3 = 0.109. Therefore, x3 should be removed, and now only
x1 remains. Thus, the ranking is x1 ≻ x3 ≻ x2, just as expected.
Now consider a real dataset: the concrete compressive strength dataset from Yeh (1998)
which can be obtained from the UCI Machine Learning Repository (Dua and Graff, 2017).
This dataset has N = 1, 030 rows with eight continuous predictors pertaining to concrete
ingredients and age, and one response: compressive strength. First, we split the dataset
into training and testing sets in the ratio 95:5 using SPlit (Vakayil et al., 2022). Then we
fit a random forest model and a Gaussian process model (OK) with Gaussian correlation
function to the training data with the full set of eight predictors and using the predictors
selected using FIRST (about four factors). The random forest was fitted using the R package
randomForest (Liaw and Wiener, 2002) and the GP model using rkriging (Huang and
Joseph, 2024). The root mean squared prediction errors (RMSE) are then computed on
the testing set. This process is repeated 30 times and the resulting RMSEs are shown as
boxplots in Figure 11.2. Although the RMSEs are higher with the selected set of predictors
compared to the full set of predictors, this might be a reasonable performance considering
that only about half the predictors have been used.

Twin-Gaussian Process 201
11.2 Twin-Gaussian Process
Consider the Gaussian process regression problem that we discussed in Section 2.2.3:
y = h(x) + ϵ, h(x)∼ GP (µ, τ2R(·)), ϵ
iid
∼ N(0, σ2),
but this time with large amounts of data ( XN , YN ) = {(X(i), Y(i))}N
i=1. For example, in
Chapter 10, we saw the wind power data (Ding, 2019) that had N = 39, 195 points. It
was too big for a Gaussian process model, and therefore, we obtained a subsample of 392
points using support points and fitted the GP model. The fitting on the subsample using
rkriging took only about 0.3 seconds in a 2.10 GHz desktop computer. Since we know
that the computational time for fitting a GP scales at O(N 3), we can estimate the time it
would have taken to fit on the full dataset as 0 .3 × (39195/392)3, which is approximately
3.5 days! Thus, it is computationally expensive to fit a GP on this full dataset. However, as
shown in Figure 10.2, the fitted GP model on the subsample is not good. Therefore, there
is a need to develop GP to fit on large datasets, which has been an active area of research
in statistics and machine learning (Gramacy, 2020, Ch. 9).
The computational bottleneck of GP comes from the need to take the inverse and de-
terminant of the N × N correlation matrix R. Therefore, some approaches try to simplify
these computations using sparse matrix techniques with compactly-supported correlation
functions (Furrer et al., 2006; Kaufman et al., 2011), but it compromises GP’s prediction
performance. Another approach is to partition the data region into smaller regions, fit GPs
in each of the subregions, and then combine the predictions (Tresp, 2000; Gramacy and
Lee, 2008; Cao and Fleet, 2014), but they can have discontinuities in the prediction. Park
and Apley (2018) try to overcome the discontinuity issue using pseudo-observations at the
boundaries of the subregions at the expense of increased computational cost. Another ap-
proach to dealing with the large dataset is to choose a small representative subsample from
the full region, and fit a global GP on the subsample, which is the idea we used for the wind
power dataset. This approach is closely related to sparse GP methods (Qui˜ nonero-Candela
and Rasmussen, 2005; Snelson and Ghahramani, 2005; Titsias, 2009). An improvement
to this approach is to build GPs on several subsamples and average them (Tresp, 2000;
Deisenroth and Ng, 2015; Li and Mak, 2025). Gramacy and Apley (2015) proposed choos-
ing a subset of data points depending on the prediction location and fit a local GP. Another
popular approach uses Vecchia’s approximation (Vecchia, 1988), where likelihood is factor-
ized into a product of univariate conditional distributions based on some ordering of the
data (Katzfuss and Guinness, 2021).
Since the global and local subset of points can be obtained using experimental design
methods, here we will explain the approach of Vakayil and Joseph (2022a), which combines
the global and local GP approximation methods. We can use twinning discussed in Section
10.3 to get a good and fast subsample from a large dataset. We can then build a global
GP using this subsample. However, prediction errors can be large, as shown in Figure 10.2.
Suppose that we want to predict the power when the wind speed is around 20 m/s. There
are many data points around this value and we could have used them to develop a better
prediction model. One way to do this is to obtain the residuals of the global GP and fit a
local GP using only the points around 20 m/s. We can then add the prediction from the
local GP to the global GP prediction to get the final prediction. Ba and Joseph (2012) have
shown that such a two-stage GP predictor is equivalent to modeling the underlying function
as the sum of two GPs, which is again equivalent to fitting a single GP with a correlation
function of the form:
R(∆) = (1 − λ)G(∆) + λL(∆), (11.8)

--- Page 215 ---
202 Data Analysis
where G(·) is the correlation function for the global GP, L(·) the correlation function for
the local GP, and λ ∈ [0, 1] is a weight factor to balance the predictions from the global
and local GPs. Due to the twin datasets Dg and Dl, and twin correlation functions G(·)
and L(·), Vakayil and Joseph (2024a) call this method TwinGP.
Denote the ng global training points obtained using twinning as Dg = {xi}ng
i=1 and the
l local training points obtained around the prediction point x as Dl(x) = {XL(x)}, where
L(x) denotes the set of local point indices. Gramacy and Apley (2015) describe sequential
design methods to carefully select local points, but we avoid this step to save computational
time. Instead, given the testing location x, we obtain Dl as the l nearest neighbors to x in
{Xi}N
i=1 \ Dg, a task that can be performed efficiently with kd-tree in O(l log l). We will
now fit a GP on the combined set Dc(x) = Dg ∪ Dl(x), which contains n = ng + nl points.
For notational simplicity, we will denote Dl(x) by Dl and Dc(x) by Dc.
Let Yc be the n × 1 vector of response values corresponding to Dc. Thus, given Yc, we
can obtain the posterior distribution as
h(x)|Yc ∼ N(ˆy(x), s2(x)),
where
ˆy(x) = ˆµn + rc(x)′(Rcc + ηIn)−1(Yc − ˆµn1n),
s2(x) = τ 2
n

1 + η − rc(x)′(Rcc + ηIn)−1rc(x)

become expensive if the testing set is large. However, this computation can be simplified as
follows. First, partition the matrix Rcc as

Consider again the wind power dataset with N = 39, 195 points. TwinGP is implemented
in the R package twingp (Vakayil and Joseph, 2024b). By default, it uses ng ≈
√
N and

1,000-point equally-spaced testing set is shown in Figure 11.3. Although not as smooth as
the predictions in Figure 10.2, the accuracy is much better. The overall fitting and prediction
took less than 0.20 seconds, which clearly shows that TwinGP can be used efficiently to fit
GPs with large datasets.

1. Let X = (X1, . . . , Xp) ∈ X ⊆Rp be a p-dimensional random vector with joint distribu-
tion PX and Y = h(XA∗ ) ∈ Y ⊆R be a scalar output depending only on the input XA∗
where A∗ ⊆ 1 :p is the set of indices of the important factors, i.e., Stot
i > 0 for i ∈ A∗ and

Stot
j = 0 for j /∈ A∗. Let A ⊆ A∗ and VA be the model variance that can be explained

i > 0 (Huang
and Joseph, 2025a). This justifies the use of forward selection using ˆVu in (11.7).
2.Consider the coefficient proposed by Azadkia and Chatterjee (2021) to quantify the
degree of conditional dependence between Y and Z given X:
ξ(Y, Z|X) =
R
E{Var(E[1Y ≥t|Z, X]|X)}dµ(t)R
E[Var(1Y ≥t|X)]dµ(t) ,
where µ is the law ofY . This coefficient is bounded between 0 and 1, with 0 indicating that
Y and Z are conditionally independent given X, and 1 if and only ifY is some measurable
function of Z given X. Consider the case of binary output Y = h(Xu, X∼u) ∈ {0, 1}.
Show that the numerator of ξ(Y, Xu|X∼u) is the same as the numerator of the total
Sobol’ index (Huang and Joseph, 2025a):
Z
E{Var(E[1Y ≥t|Xu, X∼u]|X∼u)}dµ(t) = V tot
u .
This result shows that FIRST can be used for factor selection and ranking in classification
problems as well.
3.Consider the meat spectroscopy data of 215 samples given in the R package faraway
(Faraway, 2025). For each sample, the fat content was measured along with a 100-channel
spectrum of absorbances. Since determining the fat content via analytical chemistry is
time consuming, it will be good to build a model to predict the fat content of new
samples using the 100 absorbances which can be measured more easily. Split the dataset
in 80:20 ratio and fit a random forest and XGBoost on the training set. Compare their
prediction performance on the testing set with the full set of 100 factors and using the
factors selected by FIRST. Repeat this procedure 30 times and show the results using
boxplots.
4.Consider the ozone column dataset which contains measurement of total column of ozone
by the NIMBUS-7/TOMS satellite on October 1, 1988, at different latitudes and longi-
tudes over the globe. There are 182 , 591 observations with d = 2 inputs, latitude and
longitude. The response is the total column of ozone. The data is available in the file
ozone.csv. Split the data in 90:10 ratio and fit the following GP models on the training
set.
(a)laGP (Gramacy and Apley, 2015)
(b)TwinGP (Vakayil and Joseph, 2024a).
Compute the root mean squared prediction error and the interval score in (2.53) with
α = 0.05 on the testing set. Repeat the procedure 30 times and compare the performance
of the two methods, including their computational time.
